<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
For each unit vector $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, define $\Phi_\omega\colon\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:quadratic-tests}
\Phi_\omega(x)\vcentcolon=\frac12|P_\omega x|^2,
\quad\text{with}\quad
P_\omega y\vcentcolon=y-(\omega\cdot y)\omega.
\end{equation}
We call $t\Phi_\omega|_{\partial\Omega}$, $t>0$,
\emph{cylindrical boundary data}.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:cylindrical}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and let $f_1,f_2\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative. For $j=1,2$, $\omega\in \mathbb S^{n-1}$, and $t>0$, let $u_{j,t}^{\omega}\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ be the unique Alexandrov solution of
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:MA-main-thm}
\begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f_j\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     u=t\Phi_\omega
 \quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega,
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
provided by Proposition~\ref{prop:forward}. Suppose that, for every $\omega\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$, there is a sequence $(t_k^\omega)_{k\in\mathbb N}\subset(0,\infty)$ with $t_k^\omega\to\infty$ such that the boundary slopes corresponding to the cylindrical data $t_k^\omega \Phi_\omega$ agree:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:countably-equal-slopes}
 q_{u_{1,t_k^\omega}^{\omega}}  =q_{u_{2,t_k^\omega}^{\omega}}
 \quad\text{on }\partial\Omega,
 \qquad\text{for every }k\in{\mathbb N};
\end{equation}
see Proposition~\ref{def:slope}. Then $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and let $f_1,f_2\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative. Assume that the global Cauchy data sets agree:
\[
   \mathcal C_{f_1}^{\mathrm{glob}}=\mathcal C_{f_2}^{\mathrm{glob}};
\]
see Proposition~\ref{def:gen-cauchy-data}. Then $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:Linfty}
 Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and let $f_1,f_2\in L^\infty (\Omega)$ be nonnegative.  If $\Lambda_{f_1}^-=\Lambda_{f_2}^-$ pointwise, that is,
\[
\Lambda_{f_1}^-(\varphi)(x)
=
\Lambda_{f_2}^-(\varphi)(x),\quad x\in\partial\Omega,
\]
for every $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$, then $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:weighted-MA-uniqueness}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$, $n\geq2$, be a bounded uniformly
convex $C^{1,1}$ domain, and let
$\vartheta\colon\mathbb R^n\to(0,\infty)$ be a known locally
integrable Borel function satisfying
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bounds-weight-vartheta}
\vartheta(p)\geq c_\vartheta>0
\qquad\text{for a.e. }p\in\mathbb R^n.
\end{equation}
Let $f_1,f_2\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative. For
$j=1,2$, $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, and $t>0$, suppose that
$u_{j,t}^\omega\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is an Alexandrov solution of
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted-MA-main}
\begin{cases}
\vartheta(\nabla u)\det D^2u=f_j
&\text{in }\Omega,\\
u=t\Phi_\omega
&\text{on }\partial\Omega.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Assume that, for every $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, there exists a
sequence $(t_k^\omega)_{k\in\mathbb N}\subset(0,\infty)$ with
$t_k^\omega\to\infty$ such that
\[
q_{u_{1,t_k^\omega}^\omega}
=
q_{u_{2,t_k^\omega}^\omega}
\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega,
\qquad k\in\mathbb N.
\]
Then $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
We note that the outer regularity condition \eqref{eq:outer-regular}, in particular, ensures that $\mu$ is Borel regular. Recall that in the case $X={\mathbb R}^n$, every locally finite Borel regular measure is automatically a Radon measure \cite[Chapter~1, Theorem~4]{evans-mt}.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
An important example of a Borel regular measure is the $s$-dimensional Hausdorff measure $\mathcal{H}^s$ (see~\cite[Section~2]{evans-mt}). For $s\geq 0$ and $A\subset{\mathbb R}^n$, it is defined as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Hausdorff-measure}
    \mathcal{H}^s(A)\vcentcolon = \lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{H}^s_{\delta}(A)=\sup_{0<\delta<\infty}\mathcal{H}^s_{\delta}(A),
\end{equation}
where
\[
    \mathcal{H}^s_{\delta}(A)\vcentcolon = \inf\left\{\sum_{j\geq 1}\omega_s\left(
    \frac{\operatorname{diam}C_j}{2}\right)^s : A\subset \bigcup_{j\geq 1}C_j,\,\operatorname{diam} C_j\leq \delta\right\},
\]
with normalization
\[
    \omega_s\vcentcolon =\frac{\pi^{s/2}}{\Gamma(s/2+1)}.
\]
Here, $\Gamma(z)=\int_0^{\infty} t^{z-1}e^{-t}\,dt$, $0<z<\infty$, is the usual Gamma function. In contrast to the Lebesgue measure, $\mathcal{H}^s$ is not a Radon measure for $0\leq s<n$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lemma:convex-dom}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded open convex set.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item\label{hyperplances-conv} For every $x\in \partial\Omega$, there exists a supporting hyperplane of $\overline{\Omega}$ containing $x$.
        \item\label{convex-c1-dom} Assume, in addition, that $\partial\Omega$ is of class $C^1$ and, for every $x\in\partial\Omega$, define the affine tangent hyperplane
        \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:affine-tangent-plane}
            H_x \vcentcolon= x+T_x\partial\Omega = x+\nu(x)^\perp
            = \left\{ y\in\mathbb R^n: \nu(x)\cdot(y-x)=0 \right\}.
        \end{equation}
        Then $H_x$ is the unique supporting hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$ containing $x$. Moreover,
        \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:left-tangent-plane}
            \overline\Omega \subset
            \left\{ y\in\mathbb R^n: \nu(x)\cdot(y-x)\leq0 \right\}.
        \end{equation}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The first assertion \ref{hyperplances-conv} is an immediate consequence of the Hahn--Banach theorem \cite[Theorem~1.6]{Brezis} with $A=\Omega$ and $B=\{x\}$. 

    Thus, it remains to establish \ref{convex-c1-dom}. Fix $x\in\partial\Omega$, and let $H$ be a supporting hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$ containing $x$, whose existence follows from assertion~\ref{hyperplances-conv}. There exist
$q\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ and $\alpha\in\mathbb R$ such that
\[
H
=
[\ell_q=\alpha],\quad\text{with}\quad \ell_q(y)=q\cdot y.
\]
Replacing $(q,\alpha)$ by $(-q,-\alpha)$ if necessary, we may assume
that
\[
\overline\Omega
\subset
[\ell_q\leq \alpha].
\]
Since $x\in H$, we have $\alpha=q\cdot x$, and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:supporting-normal-m}
q\cdot(y-x)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in\overline\Omega.
\end{equation}

Now, let $\tau\in T_x\partial\Omega$. Since $\partial\Omega$ is of class $C^1$, there exists a $C^1$ curve $\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\partial\Omega$ such that
\[
\gamma(0)=x,
\qquad
\gamma'(0)=\tau.
\]
By \eqref{eq:supporting-normal-m},
\[
q\cdot(\gamma(t)-x)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for every }|t|<\varepsilon.
\]
Dividing by $t>0$ and letting $t\downarrow0$ gives $q\cdot\tau\leq0$, whereas dividing by $t<0$ and letting $t\uparrow0$ gives $q\cdot\tau\geq0$.
Hence $q\cdot\tau=0$. Since this holds for every $\tau\in T_x\partial\Omega$, the vector $q$ is orthogonal to $T_x\partial\Omega$. Because $q$ and $\nu(x)$ are unit vectors, $q=\pm\nu(x)$.

To determine the sign, recall that, since $\partial\Omega$ is of class $C^1$ and $\nu(x)$ is the outward unit normal, there exists $\varepsilon_0>0$ such that
\[
x-s\nu(x)\in\Omega
\qquad\text{for every }0<s<\varepsilon_0.
\] 
If $q=-\nu(x)$, then
\[
q\cdot\bigl((x-s\nu(x))-x\bigr)=s>0,
\]
contradicting \eqref{eq:supporting-normal-m}. Hence $q=\nu(x)$. Consequently,
\[
H
=
\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)=0\}
=
H_x,
\]
and \eqref{eq:supporting-normal-m} becomes
\[
\overline\Omega
\subset
\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)\leq0\},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:left-tangent-plane}.

Since the preceding argument applies to every supporting hyperplane
of $\overline\Omega$ containing $x$, each such hyperplane equals
$H_x$. Thus $H_x$ is unique.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:strictly-convex-domains}
    An open convex set $\Omega$ is called
\emph{strictly convex} if every supporting hyperplane of
$\overline\Omega$ intersects $\overline\Omega$ at exactly one point.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:supporting-hyperplanes}
    Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded convex domain with $C^1$ boundary. Then Definition~\ref{def:strictly-convex-domains} and property \eqref{eq:strictly-convex} are equivalent.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Suppose first that $\Omega$ is strictly convex in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:strictly-convex-domains}. Fix
$x\in\partial\Omega$ and let $H_x=x+T_x\partial\Omega$. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:convex-dom}\ref{convex-c1-dom}, $H_x$ is a
supporting hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$ containing $x$. Hence,
Definition~\ref{def:strictly-convex-domains} implies that
\[
H_x\cap\overline\Omega=\{x\}.
\]
On the other hand, \eqref{eq:left-tangent-plane} gives
\[
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)\leq0
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in\overline\Omega.
\]
If equality held for some
$y\in\overline\Omega\setminus\{x\}$, then $y\in H_x$, contradicting
$H_x\cap\overline\Omega=\{x\}$. Therefore
\[
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)<0
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in\overline\Omega\setminus\{x\}.
\]
Since $x\in\partial\Omega$ was arbitrary, \eqref{eq:strictly-convex}
holds.

Conversely, suppose that $\Omega$ satisfies
\eqref{eq:strictly-convex}, and let $H$ be any supporting
hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$. By definition, $H\cap\overline\Omega\neq\varnothing$. Choose $x\in H\cap\overline\Omega$. Necessarily $x\in\partial\Omega$, since every hyperplane through an interior point of $\Omega$ has points of $\Omega$ on both sides. By Lemma~\ref{lemma:convex-dom}\ref{convex-c1-dom}, $H_x$ is the unique
supporting hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$ containing $x$. Hence $H=H_x$. Now \eqref{eq:strictly-convex} gives
\[
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)<0
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in\overline\Omega\setminus\{x\}.
\]
Consequently, no point of
$\overline\Omega\setminus\{x\}$ belongs to $H_x$, and therefore
\[
H\cap\overline\Omega
=
H_x\cap\overline\Omega
=
\{x\}.
\]
Thus every supporting hyperplane of $\overline\Omega$ intersects
$\overline\Omega$ at exactly one point, so $\Omega$ is strictly
convex in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:strictly-convex-domains}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
Corollary~\ref{cor:supporting-hyperplanes} shows that, for a strictly
convex domain with $C^1$ boundary, every boundary point is exposed.
In particular, the boundary of such a domain contains no nontrivial
line segment.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:subdiff}
Let $u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb R$ be convex. For $x\in\Omega$, the
\emph{subdifferential of $u$ at $x$} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subdifferential}
\partial u(x)
\vcentcolon=
\left\{
p\in\mathbb R^n:
u(y)\geq u(x)+p\cdot(y-x)
\text{ for every }y\in\Omega
\right\}.
\end{equation}
The elements of $\partial u(x)$ are called \emph{subgradients} of
$u$ at $x$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:rel-subgrad-grad}
If $u$ is differentiable at $x\in\Omega$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:classical-der-at-diff-points}
\partial u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)\}.
\end{equation}
Conversely, if $\partial u(x)$ is a singleton for some point $x\in \Omega$, then $u$ is differentiable at this point and $\partial u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)\}$. This follows from \cite[Theorem~25.1]{Rockafellar}. Thus the subdifferential is a
natural set-valued extension of the classical gradient.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:diff-convex}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set and suppose $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ is convex. Then $u$ is locally Lipschitz continuous and
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Lipschitz}
        \|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\leq \frac{2\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega')}}{\operatorname{dist}(K,\partial\Omega')}
    \end{equation}
    for every $K\Subset\Omega'\Subset \Omega$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:unique-subgradients}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set. Suppose that $v\colon \Omega \to {\mathbb R}$ is convex. Then the set of non-unique subgradients
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:equal-subgradients}
        Z_v\vcentcolon = \{p\in{\mathbb R}^n:p\in \partial v(x)\cap \partial v(y)\text{ for some }x\neq y\in \Omega\}
    \end{equation}
    has Lebesgue measure zero.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{proof-unique-subgradients}
    When $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ is bounded, then this result follows from Rademacher's theorem \cite[Section~3.1]{evans-mt} applied to the \emph{Legendre transform} of $v$, defined as the map $v^*\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to{\mathbb R}\cup\{\infty\}$ with
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Legendre}
     v^*(p)\vcentcolon = \sup_{x\in\Omega}(x\cdot p-v(x)),\quad p\in{\mathbb R}^n.
    \end{equation}
    It can indeed be invoked, since the boundedness of $\Omega$ implies the Lipschitz continuity of $v^*$. If $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ is a general open convex set, then one writes $\Omega=\bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb N}} \Omega_k$ with $\Omega_k=\Omega\cap B_k$. Since the bounded case applies to 
    \[
         Z^k_v\vcentcolon = \{p\in{\mathbb R}^n:p\in \partial v(x)\cap \partial v(y)\text{ for some }x\neq y\in \Omega_k\},
    \]
    the inclusion $Z_v\subset \bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb N}} Z_v^k$ completes the proof.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lemma:compactness-subgradinets}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex domain and suppose $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ is a convex function. For any compact set $K\subset\Omega$, the image $\partial u(K)$ is compact. If, in addition, the compact set $K$ is nonempty, then $\partial u(K)\neq \varnothing$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The first part follows from \cite[Lemma~A.22]{Figalli}. The second part follows from \cite[Theorem~24.7]{Rockafellar}, the fact that a proper convex function is closed if and only if it is lower semicontinuous, and Lemma~\ref{lem:diff-convex}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:gradient-convergence}
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex domain. Suppose $u_k\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ and $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ are convex.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item\label{conv-subgrad-1} If $u_k\to u$ locally uniformly, and the sequences $x_k\in\Omega$, $p_k\in\partial u_k(x_k)$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, satisfy
    \[
        x_k\to x\in\Omega\quad\text{and}\quad p_k\to p, \quad k\to\infty,
    \]
    then $p\in\partial u(x)$.
    \item\label{conv-subgrad-2}  If $u_k\to u$ locally uniformly, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:convergence-of-gradients}
  \nabla u_k(x)\to\nabla u(x),\qquad \text{as }k\to\infty
\end{equation}
    for a.e.~$x\in\Omega$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
\ref{conv-subgrad-1}: For each $k\in{\mathbb N}$, we have
\[
 u_k(z)\geq u_k(x_k)+p_k\cdot(z-x_k),\quad \text{for all }z\in\Omega.
\]
Since the convergence $u_k\to u$ is uniform on compact sets and $u$ is continuous, we have $u_{\ell}(x_{\ell})\to u(x)$, as $\ell\to\infty$. Thus passing to the limit $k\to\infty$ yields
\[
 u(z)\geq u(x)+p\cdot(z-x),\quad  \text{for all }z\in\Omega.
\]
Hence $p\in\partial u(x)$. 

\ref{conv-subgrad-2}: We first show that \eqref{eq:convergence-of-gradients} holds for every point in which $u_k$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, and $u$ are differentiable. Suppose that $x\in \Omega$ is such a differentiablity point. Set $p_k:=\nabla u_k(x)$, for $k\in{\mathbb N}$.
We first show that $(p_k)_{k\in{\mathbb N}}$ is bounded. Choose $r>0$ such that
$\overline B_r(x)\subset\Omega$. Local uniform convergence implies
that there exists $M>0$ such that
\[
|u_k(y)|\leq M
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in\overline B_r(x)
\]
and every sufficiently large $k$. If $p_k\neq0$, apply the
subgradient inequality with
\[
y_k:=x+r\frac{p_k}{|p_k|}\in\overline B_r(x).
\]
Then
\[
M
\geq
u_k(y_k)
\geq
u_k(x)+p_k\cdot(y_k-x)
=
u_k(x)+r|p_k|
\geq
-M+r|p_k|.
\]
Consequently, $(p_k)_{k\in{\mathbb N}}$ is bounded and every subsequence of $(p_k)_{k\in{\mathbb N}}$ has a further convergent subsequence. If $p_{k_j}\to q$, the first part of the proof, applied with $x_{k_j}=x$, shows that $q\in\partial u(x)$. Since $u$ is differentiable at $x$, Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad} gives $q=\nabla u(x)$. Thus every convergent subsequence of $(p_k)_{k\in{\mathbb N}}$ converges to the same limit $\nabla u(x)$. It follows that the entire sequence converges to $\nabla u(x)$, which establishes \eqref{eq:convergence-of-gradients} for all common differentiability points of $u_k$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, and $u$.

Finally, by Lemma~\ref{lem:diff-convex}, the functions $u_k$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, and $u$ are locally Lipschitz in $\Omega$. Thus, Rademacher's theorem \cite[Section~3.1]{evans-mt} implies that the function $u_k,u$ are differentiable almost everywhere. Hence, the convergence \eqref{eq:convergence-of-gradients} holds for a.e.~$x\in\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:uniformly-convex-domains}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded $C^1$ domain. Then $\Omega$ is called \emph{uniformly convex}, if there exists a constant $\kappa_\Omega>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:geometric-uniform-convexity}
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)
\leq
-\kappa_\Omega|y-x|^2
\qquad
\text{for all }x\in\partial\Omega
\text{ and }y\in\overline\Omega,
\end{equation}
where $\nu\in C^0(\partial\Omega)$ is the outward pointing unit normal field to $\partial\Omega$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:defining-function}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded domain with $C^{1,1}$
boundary. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item\label{unif-convex-1} $\Omega$ is uniformly convex in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:uniformly-convex-domains}.
\item\label{unif-convex-2} There exist an open convex neighborhood $U$ of $\overline\Omega$, a function $\rho\in C^{1,1}(U)$, and a constant $c_0>0$ such that $\rho$ is \emph{uniformly convex} with constant $c_0$, meaning that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:uniform-convex-fct}
    D^2\rho\geq c_0I\quad\text{a.e. in }U,
\end{equation}
and $\overline{\Omega}$ can be represented as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:strong-defining-function}
    \Omega=\{x\in U:\rho(x)<0\}\quad\text{and}\quad
\partial\Omega=\{x\in U: \rho(x)=0\}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
In fact, under \ref{unif-convex-1}, one may take
$U=\mathbb R^n$. Moreover, every defining function $\rho$ satisfying \ref{unif-convex-2} obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rep-normal}
\nu(x)
=
\frac{\nabla\rho(x)}{|\nabla\rho(x)|},
\qquad x\in\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:Figalli-defining-function}
 We point out that in the smooth
setting, \cite[Section~3.1.2, especially footnote~1 on
pp.~40--41]{Figalli} constructs a uniformly convex function
vanishing on $\partial\Omega$ by first using
\[
-\operatorname{dist}(\,\cdot\,,\partial\Omega)
+\operatorname{dist}(\,\cdot\,,\partial\Omega)^2
\]
near the boundary and then extending it as a uniformly convex
function through the interior. Lemma~\ref{lem:defining-function} gives the corresponding $C^{1,1}$ defining-function characterization used here.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lemma:Area-formula-grad}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set and assume that $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$ is convex. Then, for every Lebesgue measurable set $A\subset\Omega$,
    \begin{equation}
\label{eq:area-formula-gradient}
    \int_A \det D^2 u\,dx= \mathcal{L}^n(\nabla u(A)).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
First, by  Kirszbraun's theorem \cite[Section~2.10.34]{federer2014geometric}, we may choose a Lipschitz function $V\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to{\mathbb R}^n$ such that $V=\nabla u$ on $\overline{\Omega}$. Thus, applying the area formula \eqref{eq:area-formula} with $F=V$, we get
    \[   
    \int_{A}JV\,dx=\int_{{\mathbb R}^n}\mathcal{H}^0(A\cap V^{-1}(y))\,d\mathcal{H}^n(y).
    \]
    Since $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$ is convex, we deduce that $D^2u$ is symmetric and nonnegative for a.e.~$x\in\Omega$. Therefore, 
    \[
        JV=|\det DV|=|\det D^2 u|=\det D^2 u\quad\text{in }\Omega.
    \]
    Thus, $A\subset \Omega$, $V=\nabla u$ in $\Omega$, and $\mathcal{H}^n=\mathcal{L}^n$ on ${\mathbb R}^n$ \cite[Section~2.2]{evans1991measure}, imply that
     \[   \int_{A}\det D^2 u\,dx=\int_{{\mathbb R}^n}\mathcal{H}^0(A\cap (\nabla u)^{-1}(y))\,dy.
    \]
    The right hand side can be simplified as follows
    \[
        \begin{split}
            \int_{{\mathbb R}^n}\mathcal{H}^0(A\cap (\nabla u)^{-1}(y))\,dy&=\int_{{\mathbb R}^n}\mathcal{H}^0(\{x\in A: \nabla u(x)=y\})\,dy\\
            &=\int_{\nabla u(A)}\mathcal{H}^0(\{x\in A: \nabla u(x)=y\})\,dy\\
            &=\mathcal{L}^n(\nabla u(A)),
        \end{split}
    \]
    where in the last step we applied Lemma~\ref{lem:unique-subgradients}. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:MA-measure}
    Let $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ be a convex function, defined on a convex open set $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$. Then the \emph{Monge--Ampère measure} induced by $u$ is defined as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:MA-measure}
  \mu_u\colon 2^\Omega \to [0,\infty],\quad \mu_u(A)=\mathcal L^n(\partial u(A)).
\end{equation}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:MA-measure}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set. Assume that $u\colon\Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ is convex and let $\mu_u$ be the Monge--Amp\`ere measure defined by \eqref{eq:MA-measure}.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item\label{general-case} The set function $\mu_u$ is a locally finite Borel measure on $\Omega$.
        \item\label{C1-case} If $u\in C^1(\Omega)$, then $\mu_u$ is a Radon measure.
        \item\label{C11-case} If $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$, then $\mu_u=(\det D^2u)\,dx$ with $\det D^2u\in L^\infty(\Omega)$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
\ref{general-case}: We first show that $\mu_u$ is an (outer) measure. Clearly, $\mu_u(\varnothing)=0$. The $\sigma$-subadditivity of $\mu_u$ follows from the identity
\[
    \partial u \left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty} A_j\right)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{\infty}\partial u(A_j)
\]
and the $\sigma$-subadditivity of $\mathcal{L}^n$. Thus $\mu_u$ is a measure.

We next prove that every Borel subset of $\Omega$ is
$\mu_u$-measurable. Introduce the exceptional set
\[
Z_u
\vcentcolon=
\left\{
p\in\mathbb R^n:
p\in\partial u(x)\cap\partial u(y)
\text{ for some }x,y\in\Omega,\ x\neq y
\right\}.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:unique-subgradients}, $\mathcal L^n(Z_u)=0$. Moreover, the proof of \cite[Theorem~2.3, Step~1]{Figalli} shows that $\partial u(A)$ is Lebesgue measurable whenever $A\subset\Omega$ is Borel.

Let $S\subset\Omega $ be arbitrary and let $A\subset\Omega $ be Borel. We need to verify that
\[
    \mu_u(S)=\mu_u(A\cap S)+\mu_u(S\setminus A).
\]
To this end, set
\[
C\vcentcolon=\partial u(S\cap A),
\qquad
D\vcentcolon=\partial u(S\setminus A),
\qquad
M\vcentcolon=\partial u(A).
\]
Then
\[
\partial u(S)=C\cup D,
\quad 
C\subset M, \quad\text{and}\quad D\cap M\subset Z_u.
\]
By \cite[Theorem~2.3, Step~1]{Figalli}, $M$ is Lebesgue measurable. Setting $D_0=D\setminus M$, we have
\[
\mathcal L^n(D_0)\leq \mathcal L^n(D)=\mathcal L^n(D\setminus M)+\mathcal L^n(D\cap M)\leq \mathcal L^n(D_0)+\mathcal L^n(Z_u)=\mathcal L^n(D_0).
\]
Similarly,
\[
\mathcal L^n(C\cup D_0)\leq \mathcal L^n(C\cup D)\leq \mathcal L^n(C\cup D_0)+\mathcal L^n(D\cap M)=\mathcal L^n(C\cup D_0).
\]
Since $C\subset M$ and $D_0\subset {\mathbb R}^n\setminus M$, the $\mathcal{L}^n$-measurablity of $M$ gives
\[
    \mathcal L^n(C\cup D_0)=\mathcal L^n(C)+\mathcal L^n(D_0), 
\]
because $(C\cup D_0)\cap M=C$ and $(C\cup D_0)\setminus M=D_0$. Therefore,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mu_u(S)
&=\mathcal L^n(C\cup D)\\
&=\mathcal L^n(C\cup D_0)\\
&=\mathcal L^n(C)+\mathcal L^n(D_0)\\
&=\mathcal L^n(C)+\mathcal L^n(D)\\
&=\mu_u(S\cap A)+\mu_u(S\setminus A).
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus $A$ is $\mu_u$-measurable. Hence $\mu_u$ is a Borel (outer) measure.

Local finiteness is then immediate. If $K\Subset \Omega$ is compact, \cite[Lemma~A.22]{Figalli} implies that $\partial u(K)$ is compact, and therefore locally finiteness of $\mathcal{L}^n$ gives
\[
\mathcal L^n\bigl(\partial u(K)\bigr)
<\infty.
\]
Hence $\mu_u$ is locally finite.

\ref{C1-case}: From now on assume that $u\in C^1(\Omega)$. It remains to show that $\mu_u$ is Borel regular. First note that since $u\in C^1(\Omega)$, Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad} gives
\[
\partial u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)\},
\qquad x\in\Omega.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\mu_u(C)=\mathcal L^n(\nabla u(C))
\qquad\text{for every }C\subset\Omega.
\] 
Now, let $A\subset \Omega$ be an arbitrary subset. Since $\mathcal{L}^n$ is Borel regular, there exists a Borel set $B\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ such that $\nabla u(A)\subset B$ and $\mathcal{L}^n(B)=\mu_u(A)$. The continuity of $\nabla u$ implies that $S\vcentcolon = (\nabla u)^{-1}(B)$ is a Borel set. Since
\[
    A\subset (\nabla u)^{-1}((\nabla u)(A))\subset (\nabla u)^{-1}(B)=S
\]
and
\[
    (\nabla u)(S)=(\nabla u)(\nabla u)^{-1}(B)\subset B,
\]
we get
\[
    \mu_u(A)\leq \mu_u(S)\leq \mathcal{L}^n(B)=\mu_u(A).
\]
Thus $\mu_u(S)=\mu_u(A)$, which proves that $\mu_u$ is Borel regular. Since $\mu_u$ is also locally finite, it is a Radon measure.

\ref{C11-case}: Finally, assume that the convex function $u$ belongs to $C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$. Since $\Omega$ is a convex open set, $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$ implies that $u\in W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)$ (see \cite[Proposition~9.3]{Brezis}). Hence, $\det D^2 u\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)$. Moreover, convexity of $u$ implies that $D^2u$ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere and hence
$\det D^2u\geq0$ almost everywhere.

Since $u$ is differentiable everywhere, Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad} gives $\partial u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)\}$. Therefore Lemma~\ref{lemma:Area-formula-grad} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:equality-Lebesgue-sets}
\mu_u(A)
=
\mathcal L^n(\partial u(A))
=
\mathcal L^n(\nabla u(A))
=
\int_A\det D^2u(x)\,dx,
\end{equation}
for every Lebesgue measurable set $A\subset\Omega$.  In particular,
\[
\mu_u=(\det D^2u)\,dx
\]
as Borel measures on $\Omega$, meaning that 
\[
    \mu_u(B)
=
\int_B \det D^2u(x)\,dx\quad\text{for all Borel sets }B\subset \Omega.
\]
Hence $\mu_u\ll\mathcal L^n\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\Omega$, and its density $\det D^2 u$ belongs to $L^\infty(\Omega)$.

Let $\nu$ be the Borel regular extension of $\det D^2u\,dx$, that is, 
\[
    \nu(A)\vcentcolon = \inf \left\{\int_B \det D^2 u\,dx:A\subset B,\,B\subset\Omega\text{  Borel}\right\},\quad A\subset \Omega;
\]
cf., proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:weak-convergence-borel-measures}. Since $\det D^2u\in L^\infty(\Omega)$, $\nu$ is a Radon measure. By \ref{C1-case}, $\mu_u$ is Radon as well. Moreover, the measures $\mu_u$ and $\nu$ agree on Borel sets by
\eqref{eq:equality-Lebesgue-sets}. Hence, for every
$A\subset\Omega$, outer regularity gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mu_u(A)
&=
\inf\{\mu_u(V):A\subset V,\ V\subset\Omega\text{ open}\}\\
&=
\inf\left\{\int_V \det D^2 u\,dx:A\subset V,\ V\subset\Omega\text{ open}\right\}\\
&=
\inf\{\nu(V):A\subset V,\ V\subset\Omega\text{ open}\}\\
&=
\nu(A).
\end{aligned}
\]
Consequently, $\mu_u=(\det D^2u)\,dx$ as (outer) measures on $\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
We note that Lebesgue's decomposition theorem \cite[Section~1.6]{evans-mt} demonstrates the existence of a nonnegative function $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)$ and a Radon measure $\mu_u^{\mathrm{s}}$, singular with respect to $\mathcal L^n$, such that
\[
\mu_u(B)
=
\int_B f\,dx+\mu_u^{\mathrm{s}}(B)
\qquad
\text{for every Borel set }B\subset\Omega.
\]
Indeed, the application of this decomposition theorem can be justified rigorously in at least two ways. One possibility is to notice that 
\[
    C_c(\Omega)\ni \varphi\mapsto L_u \varphi\vcentcolon = \int_\Omega \varphi\,d\mu_u
\]
defines a continuous functional with respect to its natural locally convex topology. Indeed,
for every compact set $K\subset\Omega$,
\[
|L_u(\varphi)|
\leq
\mu_u(K)\|\varphi\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\qquad
\text{whenever }\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K.
\]
Hence, Riesz's representation theorem \cite[Section~1.8]{evans-mt} yields the existence of a unique Radon measure $\widetilde{\mu}_u$ such that
\[
    \int_\Omega \varphi\,d\mu_u=\int_\Omega \varphi\,d\widetilde{\mu}_u,\quad \varphi\in C_c(\Omega).
\]
Alternatively, the construction in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:weak-convergence-borel-measures} shows directly that $\widetilde\mu_u$ is the Borel regularization of $\mu_u$, namely
\[
    \widetilde \mu_u (A)=\widehat \mu_u(A)\vcentcolon =\inf\bigl\{
\mu_u (B):
A\subset B,\ B\subset\Omega\text{ Borel}
\bigr\},\quad A\subset \Omega.
\]
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{Example:singular-MA-measures}
The following examples illustrate two prototypical types of singular Monge--Ampère measures. In the first, the measure has an atom concentrated at a single point, whereas in the second, its singular part is supported on a hyperplane.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item  If $\Omega\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ is a convex open set containing $x_0\in{\mathbb R}^n$ and $M>0$, then 
\[
u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R},\quad u(x)=M|x-x_0|
\]
describes geometrically a cone and we have
\[
    \partial u(x)=\begin{cases}
       \big\{ M\frac{x-x_0}{|x-x_0|}\big\},\quad & x\neq x_0,\\
        \overline{B_M},\quad & x=x_0.
    \end{cases}
\]
Indeed, $p\in\partial u(x_0)$ if and only if
\[
p\cdot(z-x_0)\leq M|z-x_0|
\qquad\text{for every }z\in\Omega.
\]
Since $x_0$ is an interior point of $\Omega$, this is equivalent to $|p|\leq M$. Consequently,
\[
\mu_u
=
M^n\mathcal L^n(B_1)\,\delta_{x_0}
\] 
is a purely atomic measure, which is concentrated at the vertex $x_0$.\\

More generally, the existence theory in Section~\ref{sec:Dirichlet-theory} shows that, on a bounded convex domain, finite sums of positive Dirac measures can occur as Monge--Ampère measures.
    \item Let $e\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, $c\in\mathbb R$, and consider the hyperplane
\[
H:=\{x\in\mathbb R^n:x\cdot e=c\}.
\]
Suppose that $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ is a convex open set with $\Omega\cap H\neq \varnothing$. The convex function
\[
u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R},\quad u(x)
=
\frac12|x|^2+\frac12|x\cdot e-c|
\]
satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:MA-meas-hyper}
\mu_u
=
\mathcal L^n\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\Omega
+
\mathcal H^{n-1}\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}(H\cap\Omega).
\end{equation}
To verify this, write
\[
x_\parallel:=x\cdot e,
\qquad
x_\perp:=x-(x\cdot e)e.
\]
The subdifferential sum rule gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subgrad-hyper}
\partial u(x)
=
\begin{cases}
\{x+\frac12e\},
&x_\parallel>c,\\[1mm]
\{x-\frac12e\},
&x_\parallel<c,\\[1mm]
x+[-\frac12,\frac12]e,
&x_\parallel=c.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Indeed,
\[
\partial\frac{|x|^2}{2}=\{x\},\quad x\in{\mathbb R}^n,
\]
and
\[
\partial\frac{|x\cdot e-c|}{2}
=
\begin{cases}
\{\frac12e\},&x_\parallel>c,\\
\{-\frac12e\},&x_\parallel<c,\\
[-\frac12,\frac12]e,&x_\parallel=c.
\end{cases}
\]

Let $A\subset\Omega$ be arbitrary and set
\[
A_+:=A\cap\{x_\parallel>c\},
\qquad
A_-:=A\cap\{x_\parallel<c\},
\qquad
A_0:=A\cap H.
\]
Since $H$ and the two half-spaces determined by $H$ are Borel sets,
they are $\mu_u$-measurable. Consequently,
\[
\mu_u(A)
=
\mu_u(A_+)+\mu_u(A_-)+\mu_u(A_0).
\]
Using formula \eqref{eq:subgrad-hyper}, we find
\[
\partial u(A_{\pm})=A_{\pm}\pm\frac12e\quad\text{and} \quad \partial u(A_0)
=
A_0+\left[-\frac12,\frac12\right]e.
\]
Translation invariance of the Lebesgue measure gives
\[
\mu_u(A_+)=\mathcal L^n(A_+),
\qquad
\mu_u(A_-)=\mathcal L^n(A_-).
\]
On the other hand, in the coordinates $(x_\parallel,x_\perp)\in
\mathbb R\times e^\perp$, the set $\partial u(A_0)$ has the product representation
\[
\partial u(A_0)
=
\left[c-\frac12,c+\frac12\right]
\times
\{x_\perp:(c,x_\perp)\in A_0\}.
\]
The product formula for the Lebesgue measure therefore yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mu_u(A_0)
&=
\mathcal L^n\bigl(\partial u(A_0)\bigr)\\
&=
\mathcal L^1\left(
\left[c-\frac12,c+\frac12\right]
\right)
\mathcal H^{n-1}(A_0)\\
&=
\mathcal H^{n-1}(A_0).
\end{aligned}
\]
Combining the preceding identities, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mu_u(A)
&=
\mathcal L^n(A_+)
+\mathcal L^n(A_-)
+\mathcal H^{n-1}(A\cap H)\\
&=
\mathcal L^n(A\setminus H)
+\mathcal H^{n-1}(A\cap H)\\
&=
\mathcal L^n(A)
+\mathcal H^{n-1}(A\cap H),
\end{aligned}
\]
where the last equality follows from
$\mathcal L^n(H)=0$. Hence
\[
\mu_u
=
\mathcal L^n\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\Omega
+
\mathcal H^{n-1}\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}(H\cap\Omega)
\]
as outer measures on $\Omega$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def:alexandrov-solution}
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set. Assume that $\nu$ is a Borel measure on $\Omega$ and $\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$ is a continuous boundary value. 
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
    \item An \emph{Alexandrov solution} of
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:measure-equation}
 \det D^2u=\nu\quad\text{in }\Omega
\end{equation}
is a convex function $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alexandrov-definition}
 \mu_u=\nu
 \quad\text{as Borel measures in }\Omega,
\end{equation}
that is, $\mu_u(B)=\nu(B)$ for all Borel sets $B\subset \Omega$.

If $\nu=f\,dx$ for some measurable function
$f\colon\Omega\to[0,\infty]$, then we also say that $u$ solves
\[
\det D^2u=f
\quad\text{in }\Omega
\]
in the Alexandrov sense.
    \item An \emph{Alexandrov solution of the Dirichlet problem }
\[
\begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=\nu\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
    u=\varphi\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega
\end{cases}
\]
is a function $u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb R$ such that
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$,
\item $u$ is an Alexandrov solution of
      \eqref{eq:measure-equation}, and
\item $u=\varphi$ pointwise on $\partial\Omega$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:Alexandrov-Classica-sol}
Suppose that $\nu=f\,dx$, for some nonnegative $f\in L^1_{\rm loc}(\Omega)$. If $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline\Omega)$ is convex, then Lemma~\ref{lem:MA-measure} gives $\mu_u=(\det D^2u)\,dx$. Consequently, $u$ is an Alexandrov solution of $\det D^2u=f$ if and only if $\det D^2u=f$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$. Thus, in the $C^{1,1}$ class, the Alexandrov and almost-everywhere notions of solution agree.

In particular, every classical convex solution $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$ is an Alexandrov solution. Conversely, if an Alexandrov solution belongs to $C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$ and $f$ is continuous, then $\det D^2u=f$ pointwise in $\Omega$ and hence $u$ is a classical solution.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
Note that the convexity of an Alexandrov solution can be viewed as a consistency requirement, since a $C^2$ function $v$ is convex if and only if $D^2 v \geq 0$.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:scaling}
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a convex open set, let $\lambda>0$, let $\ell_{p,c}\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to {\mathbb R}$ be the affine function $\ell_{p,c}(x)\vcentcolon = p\cdot x+c$, and suppose that $u,v$ are convex functions on $\Omega$. The Monge--Ampère measure has the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item\label{scaling} \emph{Scaling}: $\mu_{\lambda u}=\lambda^n\mu_u$.
    \item\label{affine} \emph{Translation invariance}: $\mu_{u + \ell_{p,c} }=\mu_u$.
     \item\label{superadd} \emph{Superadditivity}: $\mu_{u+v}\geq \mu_u+\mu_v$ as Borel measures.
    \item\label{monotonicity} \emph{Monotonicity}: Let $O\subset \Omega$ be a bounded open set such that $u,v\in C(\overline{O})$. If the convex functions satisfy
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:monotonicity}
        u\leq v\text{ in }O,\quad
        u=v\text{ on }\partial O,
    \end{equation}
    then $\mu_u(O)\geq \mu_v(O)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The properties~\ref{scaling},~\ref{superadd},~and \ref{monotonicity} in the sense of Borel measures are established in \cite[Lemmas~2.7 and~2.9]{Figalli}. The scaling property \ref{scaling} and translation invariance \ref{affine} hold for all subsets $A\subset \Omega$ and not only for Borel sets, since its proof only relies on properties of the subdifferential and the scaling property and translation invariance of the Lebesgue measure, which hold for arbitrary sets; see~\cite[Section~2.1]{evans-mt}.
    
    Hence it remains to prove \ref{affine}. 
    By the sum rule for the subdifferential and Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad}, we have
    \[
        \partial (u+\ell_{p,c})(x)=\partial u(x)+\{\nabla \ell_{p,c}(x)\}=\partial u(x)+\{p\}.
    \]
    Thus, the translation invariance of the Lebesgue measure yields \ref{affine}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:weak-convergence-borel-measures}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open set. Assume that $\nu_k$, $k\in\mathbb N$, and $\nu$ are locally finite Borel measures on $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ (see~Section~\ref{sec:measures}). Then the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item $\nu_k\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup} \nu$, that is, 
\[
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\nu_k \to
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\nu
\qquad
\text{for every }\varphi\in C_c(\Omega)
\]
as $k\to\infty$.
\item\label{weak-conv-2} For every compact set $K\subset\Omega$ and every open set
$U\subset\Omega$,
\[
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\nu_k(K)\leq\nu(K)
\quad\text{and}\quad
\nu(U)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\nu_k(U).
\]
\item For every relatively compact Borel set $B\Subset\Omega$
satisfying $\nu(\partial B)=0$,
\[
\nu_k(B)\to\nu(B).
\]
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, it is enough to consider in \ref{weak-conv-2} relatively compact open sets $U\Subset \Omega$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $\eta$ be a Borel measure on $\Omega$. Define
\[
\widehat\eta\colon 2^\Omega\to[0,\infty],
\qquad
\widehat\eta(A)
:=
\inf\bigl\{
\eta(B):
A\subset B,\ B\subset\Omega\text{ Borel}
\bigr\}.
\]
We first verify that $\widehat\eta$ is a Borel regular measure.

Clearly, $\widehat\eta(\varnothing)=0$. To prove
$\sigma$-subadditivity, let $A\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty A_j$. For every $\varepsilon>0$, choose Borel sets $B_j\subset\Omega$
such that
\[
A_j\subset B_j
\quad\text{and}\quad
\eta(B_j)\leq\widehat\eta(A_j)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2^j}.
\]
Then $A\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty B_j$, and hence
\[
\widehat\eta(A)
\leq
\eta\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty B_j\right)
\leq
\sum_{j=1}^\infty\widehat\eta(A_j)+\varepsilon.
\]
Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ shows that $\widehat\eta$ is an
outer measure.

Next, every Borel set $E\subset\Omega$ is
$\widehat\eta$-measurable. Indeed, if $A\subset\Omega$ and
$B\supset A$ is Borel, then
\[
\eta(B)
=
\eta(B\cap E)+\eta(B\setminus E)
\geq
\widehat\eta(A\cap E)+\widehat\eta(A\setminus E).
\]
Taking the infimum over all such Borel sets $B$ yields
\[
\widehat\eta(A)
\geq
\widehat\eta(A\cap E)+\widehat\eta(A\setminus E).
\]
The reverse inequality follows from the $\sigma$ subadditivity of $\widehat\eta$. Thus $\widehat\eta$ is a Borel measure.

Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:regularization-agrees-on-Borel}
\widehat\eta(B)=\eta(B)
\qquad
\text{for every Borel set }B\subset\Omega.
\end{equation}
Indeed, the inequality $\widehat\eta(B)\leq\eta(B)$ follows by using $B$ itself in the defining infimum. Conversely, if $C\supset B$ is Borel, then $\eta(B)\leq\eta(C)$, and taking the infimum over $C$ gives the reverse inequality.

It remains to verify Borel regularity. Let $A\subset\Omega$. If $\widehat\eta(A)=\infty$, then
\[
\widehat\eta(\Omega)\geq\widehat\eta(A)=\infty,
\]
so that $\Omega$ is a Borel superset of $A$ having the same measure.
Suppose that $\widehat\eta(A)<\infty$. For every $j\in\mathbb N$,
choose a Borel set $C_j\subset\Omega$ such that
\[
A\subset C_j
\quad\text{and}\quad
\eta(C_j)\leq\widehat\eta(A)+\frac1j.
\]
Set
\[
B_j:=\bigcap_{\ell=1}^j C_\ell,
\qquad
B:=\bigcap_{j=1}^\infty B_j.
\]
Then $(B_j)_{j\in\mathbb N}$ is decreasing, $A\subset B$, and $\eta(B_1)<\infty$. Hence continuity from above, together with \eqref{eq:regularization-agrees-on-Borel}, gives
\[
\widehat\eta(B)
=
\eta(B)
=
\lim_{j\to\infty}\eta(B_j)
=
\widehat\eta(A).
\]
Thus $\widehat\eta$ is Borel regular. 

Furthermore, if $\eta$ is locally finite, then for every compact set $K\subset\Omega$,
\[
\widehat\eta(K)=\eta(K)<\infty.
\]
Consequently, $\widehat\eta$ is a Radon measure, whenever $\eta$ is a locally finite Borel measure.

Now, let $\widehat\nu_k$ and $\widehat\nu$
denote the Borel regularizations of $\nu_k$ and $\nu$, respectively. By \eqref{eq:regularization-agrees-on-Borel}, these Radon measures agree with the original measures on all Borel sets. In particular,
\[
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\widehat\nu_k
=
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\nu_k,
\qquad
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\widehat\nu
=
\int_\Omega\varphi\,d\nu
\]
for every $\varphi\in C_c(\Omega)$, and the regularized measures have
the same values as the original measures Borel
subsets of $\Omega$.

Therefore, the assertions follow from \cite[Section~1.9, Theorem~1]{evans-mt}, applied to $\widehat\nu_k$ and $\widehat\nu$. Although the cited theorem is stated on $\mathbb R^n$, the same proof applies on the locally compact space $\Omega$, with relatively compact Borel sets in place of bounded Borel sets.

Finally, we show that it is enough to consider in \ref{weak-conv-2} relatively compact open sets $U\Subset \Omega$. Therefore, let $U\subset \Omega$ be given and define
\[
U_j
:=
\left\{
x\in U:
\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb R^n\setminus U)>\frac1j
\right\}
\cap B_j.
\]
Then $(U_j)_{j\in\mathbb N}$ is an increasing sequence of relatively compact open sets such that
\[
U_j\Subset U
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
U=\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty U_j.
\]
Consequently, if the second condition in \ref{weak-conv-2} holds for all relatively compact open sets, then continuity from below gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\nu(U)
&=\sup_{j\in\mathbb N}\nu(U_j)\\
&\leq
\sup_{j\in\mathbb N}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\nu_k(U_j)\\
&\leq
\liminf_{k\to\infty}\nu_k(U).
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus, it holds automatically for all open subsets $U\subset \Omega$ as we wanted to demonstrate. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:weak-cont}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a convex open set. Assume that $u_k\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, and $u\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ are convex functions. If $u_k\to u$ locally uniformly as $k\to\infty$, then the Monge--Ampère measures $\mu_{u_k}$ converge weak-$*$ to $\mu_u$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:MA-measure}, the Monge--Ampère measures
$\mu_{u_k}$ and $\mu_u$ are locally finite Borel measures. The proof of \cite[Proposition~2.6]{Figalli} shows that
\[
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mu_{u_k}(K)
\leq
\mu_u(K)
\]
for every compact set $K\subset\Omega$, and
\[
\mu_u(U)
\leq
\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mu_{u_k}(U)
\]
for every open set $U\Subset\Omega$.

Hence Proposition~\ref{prop:weak-convergence-borel-measures} yields the asserted weak-$*$ convergence.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:max-principle}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a convex bounded open set and let $u\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ be a convex function that vanishes on the boundary $\partial\Omega$. There exists a dimensional constant $C_n>0$ such that
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Alexandrov-max-princ}
        |u(x)|^n\leq C_n \operatorname{diam}(\Omega)^{n-1}\mu_u(\Omega)\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
    \end{equation}
    for every $x\in\Omega$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:Comparison}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a convex open set and let $u,v\colon \Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ be convex functions. Assume that $O\subset \Omega$ is a bounded open set. If the functions $u,v\in C(\overline{O})$ satisfy
    \[
        u\geq v\text{ on }\partial O
    \]
    and
    \[
        \mu_u\leq \mu_v\quad\text{as Borel measures on }O,
    \]
    then $u\geq v$ in $O$.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:forward}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex domain in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:strictly-convex-domains}.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item\label{forward-existence}
\textit{Existence and uniqueness:}
For every finite Borel measure $\nu$ on $\Omega$ and every
$\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$, there exists a unique convex function
$u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:MA-well-posedness}
\begin{cases}
\det D^2u=\nu & \text{in }\Omega,\\
u=\varphi & \text{on }\partial\Omega
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
in the Alexandrov sense (see~Definition~\ref{def:alexandrov-solution}).

\item\label{forward-source-stability}
\textit{Stability under source perturbations:}
Let $(\nu_k)_{k\in\mathbb N}$ be finite Borel measures on $\Omega$
such that
\[
\sup_{k\in\mathbb N}\nu_k(\Omega)<\infty
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\nu_k\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}\nu\quad \text{as }k\to\infty.
\]
For a fixed $\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$, let $u_k$ and $u$ be the solutions corresponding to $(\nu_k,\varphi)$ and $(\nu,\varphi)$,
respectively. Then
\[
u_k\to u
\qquad\text{uniformly on }\overline\Omega\text{ as }k\to\infty.
\]

\item\label{forward-joint-stability}
\textit{Stability under simultaneous perturbations:}
Let $(\nu_k)_{k\in{\mathbb N}}$ be finite Borel measures satisfying the assumptions in \ref{forward-source-stability}. Let $\varphi_k,\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$, $k\in{\mathbb N}$, satisfy
\[
\varphi_k\to\varphi
\qquad\text{uniformly on }\partial\Omega\text{ as }k\to\infty.
\]
If $u_k$ is the solution corresponding to $(\nu_k,\varphi_k)$ and $u$ is the solution corresponding to $(\nu,\varphi)$, then
\[
u_k\to u
\qquad\text{uniformly on }\overline\Omega\text{ as }k\to\infty.
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
\ref{forward-existence}: By the comparison principle (Proposition~\ref{prop:Comparison}), the uniqueness assertion holds under the weaker conditions that $\Omega\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ is a convex bounded open set and $\nu$ a Borel measure. Existence of a solution to the Dirichlet problem \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness} is established in \cite[Theorem~2.14]{Figalli}.  \\
    
    \noindent \ref{forward-source-stability}: The proof of \cite[Proposition~2.16]{Figalli} shows that $u_k$ converges locally uniformly to the unique solution of the Dirichlet problem \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness}. Hence, it remains to show that the convergence holds uniformly in $\overline\Omega$. 
    
    Fix $\varepsilon>0$ and $x\in \partial\Omega$. Since $\Omega$ is a strictly convex domain, \cite[Lemma~2.15]{Figalli} yields the existence of affine functions $\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{\pm}$ satisfying
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:barriers}
       \ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{-}\leq \varphi\leq \ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{+}\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega \quad\text{and}\quad \varphi(x)-\varepsilon\leq \ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{-}(x)\leq \ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{+}(x)\leq \varphi(x)+\varepsilon.
    \end{equation}
    We observe that $u_k$ satisfies
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:barrier-est}
        w_k+\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{-}\leq u_k\leq w_k+\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^+\quad \text{in }\Omega
    \end{equation}
    where $w_k$ is the unique solution to 
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:MA-well-posedness-hom}
    \begin{cases}
        \det D^2w=\nu_k & \text{in }\Omega,\\
        w=0 & \text{on }\partial\Omega.
    \end{cases} 
    \end{equation}
    Indeed, Proposition~\ref{prop:scaling} \ref{affine} shows that $w_k+\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{\pm}$ solves \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness} with source $\nu_k$ and boundary values $\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^\pm |_{\partial\Omega}$. Thus, \eqref{eq:barrier-est} is a consequence of the comparison principle (Proposition~\ref{prop:Comparison}) and \eqref{eq:barriers}. 
    
    Moreover, by Proposition~\ref{prop:max-principle}, the convexity of $w_k$, and $w_k|_{\partial\Omega}=0$, we also have
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:two-sided-hom}
        -C \operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)^{1/n}\leq w_k(y)\leq 0\quad\text{for all }y\in\Omega.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore \eqref{eq:barrier-est} yields
    \[
        -C\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)^{1/n}+\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^{-}(y)\leq u_k (y) \leq \ell_{x,\varepsilon}^+ (y),\quad \text{for all }y\in\Omega.
    \]
   Hence, in a sufficiently small collar of the boundary we have $C\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)^{1/n}<\varepsilon$. Thus the continuity of $\ell^\pm_{x,\varepsilon}$ and \eqref{eq:barriers} yield a neighborhood $U_x \subset \overline{\Omega}$ of $x$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:barrier-uk}
    \varphi (x)-3\varepsilon \leq u_k(y) \leq \varphi(x)+2\varepsilon
\end{equation}
for all $k$ and all $y\in U_x\cap\Omega$ sufficiently close to $\partial\Omega$. Up to shrinking the neighborhood $U_x$, the same estimate holds for $u$ in place of $u_k$, since $u\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ and $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$.


By compactness of $\partial\Omega$, finitely many of the sets $U_x$ cover the boundary. Hence, there exists $\delta>0$ such that
\[
|u_k(y)-u(y)|\leq  5 \varepsilon
\]
for every $k$ whenever $\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)<\delta$. Indeed, if $u_k(y)\geq u(y)$ for some $y\in \Omega$ with $\operatorname{dist}(y,\partial\Omega)<\delta$, then it lies in some neighborhood $U_x$ and \eqref{eq:barrier-uk} implies
\[
    |u_k(y)-u(y)|=(u_k(y)-\varphi(x))-(u(y)-\varphi(x))\leq 5\varepsilon
\]
and similarly for the case $u_k(y)\leq u(y)$. Since on the compact set
\[
K_\delta
:=
\{z\in\overline\Omega:
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)\geq\delta\}
\Subset\Omega,
\]
the convergence is uniform by local uniform convergence. Therefore
\[
\limsup_{k\to\infty}
\|u_k-u\|_{L^\infty(\Omega)}
\leq5\varepsilon.
\]
Since $\varepsilon>0$ was arbitrary, $u_k\to u$ uniformly on $\overline\Omega$. This completes the proof of \ref{forward-source-stability}.\\
    
    \noindent \ref{forward-joint-stability}: Let $v_k$ denote the solution corresponding to $(\nu_k,\varphi)$ and set
\[
\varepsilon_k
:=
\|\varphi_k-\varphi\|_{L^\infty(\partial\Omega)}.
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:scaling} \ref{affine} demonstrates that $v_k\pm \varepsilon_k$ solve \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness} with source $\nu_k$ and boundary values $\varphi\pm \varepsilon_k$. Since $u_k=\varphi_k$ on $\partial\Omega$, the comparison principle (Proposition~\ref{prop:Comparison}) therefore gives
\[
v_k-\varepsilon_k
\leq u_k
\leq v_k+\varepsilon_k
\qquad\text{on }\overline\Omega.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\|u_k-v_k\|_{L^\infty(\overline\Omega)}
\leq\varepsilon_k.
\]
By assertion~\ref{forward-source-stability}, $v_k\to u$ uniformly on $\overline\Omega$, while $\varepsilon_k\to0$. Hence $u_k\to u$ uniformly on $\overline\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
As mentioned the existence of a solution to \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness} is provided by \cite[Theorem~2.14]{Figalli}, when $\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$ is arbitrary, and by \cite[Theorem~2.13]{Figalli}. Both results use the Perron method, where the subsolution set is
    \[
        \mathcal{S}[\nu]\vcentcolon = \{w\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R}:w\in C(\overline{\Omega})\text{ convex},\, w\leq \varphi\text{ on }\partial\Omega,\,\mu_w\geq \nu\text{ as Borel measures}\},
    \]
    the weak-$*$ approximation of $\nu$ by nonnegative finite linear combinations of Dirac masses,  $\sum_{n=1}^N a_n \delta_{x_n}$, $a_n\geq 0$, and the stability under source perturbations \ref{forward-source-stability}. 

    When $\varphi=0$, the source-stability assertion \ref{forward-source-stability} of Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} holds on arbitrary bounded convex domains and is a special case of \cite[Corollary~2.12]{Figalli}. The proof uses the Alexandrov maximum principle (Proposition~\ref{prop:max-principle}) to control solutions near the boundary, together with compactness, weak continuity of Monge--Ampère measures, and uniqueness.

The argument of \cite[Corollary~2.12]{Figalli} also allows varying bounded convex domains $\Omega_k$, provided\footnote{In \cite[Corollary~2.12]{Figalli}, the condition is written as $\operatorname{dist}(\partial\Omega_k,\partial\Omega)\to 0$. If $\operatorname{dist}$ denotes the usual infimum distance between sets, this condition does not imply the convergence of the domains or their diameters used in the proof: shrinking balls externally tangent to $\Omega$ give a counterexample. We therefore use Hausdorff distance, which provides the required convergence for bounded convex domains.}
\[
d_H(\partial\Omega_k,\partial\Omega)\to 0
\]
and the source measures have uniformly bounded total masses. Here, for nonempty sets $A,B\subset\mathbb R^n$,
\[
d_H(A,B):=
\max\left\{
\sup_{x\in A}\operatorname{dist}(x,B),
\sup_{y\in B}\operatorname{dist}(y,A)
\right\}.
\]
The resulting convergence of the related solutions $u_k$ is locally uniform in $\Omega$. For a fixed domain, the maximum principle estimate \eqref{eq:Alexandrov-max-princ} and the uniform boundedness of $\nu_k(\Omega)$ imply
\[
    |u_k(x)|\leq C \operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)^{1/n},\quad x\in\Omega.
\]
Hence in this case the local uniform convergence can be upgraded to uniform convergence on $\overline\Omega$.
    
    
We highlight that for the general case \eqref{eq:MA-well-posedness} the strict convexity of $\Omega$ is needed to obtain boundary barriers at every point of $\partial\Omega$, which ensure that the limiting solution attains an arbitrary prescribed datum $\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$ continuously. More precisely, they are needed in the construction of $\ell_{x,\varepsilon}^\pm$ (see~\cite[Lemma~2.15]{Figalli} and proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-source-stability}).
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:two-dimensional-regularity}
In two space dimensions, upper and lower bounds for the density of the Monge--Ampère measure impose strong geometric and regularity properties on Alexandrov solutions. Suppose that $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^2$ and that
\[
\mu_u=f\,dx
\quad\text{in }\Omega.
\]
Then the following assertions hold:
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item\label{smoothness-2d} If $f\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)$, then either
      $u\in C^1(\Omega)$ or there exists a segment $\Sigma$ crossing
      $\Omega$ along which $u$ is affine and non-differentiable; see
      \cite[Theorem~2.17]{Figalli}.
\item\label{strict-convexity-2d} If $1/f\in L^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)$, then $u$ is
      strictly convex in $\Omega$; see
      \cite[Theorem~2.19]{Figalli}.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently, if for any compact subset $K\Subset \Omega$ there exist constants $c_K,C_K>0$ such that
\[
    c_K\leq f(x)\leq C_K\quad \text{for a.e. }x\in K,
\]
then $u$ is strictly convex and belongs to $C^1(\Omega)$. Indeed, strict convexity rules out the affine-segment alternative in \ref{smoothness-2d}.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lemma:bdry-subdifferential}
    Let $\Omega\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ be a convex open set and let $u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R}$ be a convex function.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item\label{consistency} For any $x\in \Omega$, one has $\bar{\partial }u(x)=\partial u(x)$.
        \item\label{C1-functions} Assume, in addition, that $\Omega$ is a $C^1$ domain and $u\in C^1(\overline\Omega)$. Then, for every $x\in\partial\Omega$, we have
        \begin{equation}
        \label{eq:subgradient-c1}
            \bar{\partial} u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)+s\nu(x):s\geq 0\},
        \end{equation}
        where $\nu$ is the outward pointing unit normal vector field.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
\ref{consistency}: The inclusion $\bar{\partial }u(x)\subset \partial u(x)$ follows from the definitions. Now, suppose that $p\in \partial u(x)$. Then
    \[
        u(y)\geq u(x)+p\cdot (y-x)\quad\text{for all }y\in \Omega.
    \]
    Since $x\in \Omega$, this is equivalent to 
     \begin{equation}
     \label{eq:subgrad-proof}
        u(y)\geq \bar{u}(x)+p\cdot (y-x)\quad\text{for all }y\in \Omega.
    \end{equation}
    Now, let $y\in \partial \Omega$ and choose a sequence $(y_n)_{n\in{\mathbb N}}\subset \Omega$ such that
    \[
        \lim_{n\to\infty}u(y_n)=\liminf_{\Omega\ni z\to y}u(z)=\bar{u}(y).
    \]
    Using \eqref{eq:subgrad-proof} with $y=y_n$ and passing to the limit $n\to\infty$, we find
    \[
        \bar u (y)\geq \bar{u}(x)+p\cdot (y-x).
    \]
    Since $\bar u=\infty$ in ${\mathbb R}^n\setminus\overline{\Omega}$, we deduce that $p\in \bar{\partial}u(x)$. \\

    \noindent \ref{C1-functions}: Fix $x\in\partial\Omega$ and choose $x_k\in\Omega$ with $x_k\to x$. By \ref{consistency} and $u\in C^1(\Omega)$, we have $\bar{\partial}u(x_k)=\partial \bar{u}(x_k)=\{\nabla u(x_k)\}$ for each $k\in{\mathbb N}$. Thus, 
\[
 u(z)\geq u(x_k)+\nabla u(x_k)\cdot(z-x_k),\qquad z\in\Omega
\]
for each $k\in{\mathbb N}$. Therefore, since $u\in C^1(\overline{\Omega})$, passing to the limit $k\to\infty$ shows 
\[
 u(z)\geq u(x)+\nabla u(x)\cdot(z-x),\qquad z\in\Omega.
\]
By continuity of $u$ on $\overline{\Omega}$ this holds for all $z\in\overline{\Omega}$. Since $u=\bar{u}$ on $\partial\Omega$ and $\bar u=\infty$ in ${\mathbb R}^n\setminus \overline{\Omega}$, the above inequality implies that
$\nabla u(x)\in\bar{\partial} u(x)$. By \eqref{eq:left-tangent-plane} we know that
$\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)\leq 0$ for $y\in \overline{\Omega}$, and hence it follows that $\nabla u(x)+s\nu(x)\in\bar{\partial} u(x)$ for every $s\geq 0$. Therefore, 
\[
    \{\nabla u(x)+s\nu(x):s\geq 0\}\subset \bar{\partial} u(x).
\]

Conversely, let $p\in\bar{\partial} u(x)$. Choose a $C^1$ boundary curve $\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to
\partial\Omega$ with $\gamma(0)=x$ and
$\gamma'(0)=\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$. Since $p\in \bar\partial u(x)$ and $\bar u=u$ on $\partial \Omega$, we have
\[
 u(\gamma(t))-u(x)
 \geq p\cdot(\gamma(t)-x)\quad\text{for all }|t|<\varepsilon.
\]
Dividing by positive and negative $t$ and passing to zero shows
\[
 p\cdot\tau_0=\nabla_{\tau} u(x)\cdot\tau_0,
\]
where $\nabla_\tau u \vcentcolon = \nabla u- (\partial_\nu u)\nu$ is the tangential derivative of $u$ along $\partial\Omega$.

Thus, by varying $\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$, we see that every element of $\bar{\partial} u(x)$ has the form
$\nabla_\tau u(x)+a\nu(x)$.
For all sufficiently small $r>0$, the point $x-r\nu(x)$ lies in $\Omega$.
The subgradient inequality and the first-order expansion of $u$ at $x$ give
\[
 u(x)-r\nabla u(x)\cdot\nu(x)+o(r)
 =u(x-r\nu(x))\geq u(x)-ar.
\]
After division by $r$ and passage to the limit, this yields 
\[
    a\geq\nabla u(x)\cdot\nu(x)=\partial_\nu u(x).
\]
Since $\nabla u(x) =\nabla_\tau u(x)+(\partial_\nu u (x))\nu(x)$, formula \eqref{eq:subgradient-c1} follows.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{def:slope}
    Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex domain with $C^1$ boundary, let $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$, and suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. For every $x\in\partial\Omega$, define the \emph{extremal boundary slope} by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:defq}
q_u(x)
\vcentcolon=
\inf\left\{
a\in\mathbb R:
\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x)
\right\},
\end{equation}
with the convention $\inf\varnothing=+\infty$. Then
\[
q_u(x)\in(-\infty,\infty]
\qquad\text{for every }x\in\partial\Omega.
\]
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Fix $x\in\partial\Omega$. If the set appearing in
\eqref{eq:defq} is empty, then $q_u(x)=+\infty$, and there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, let $a\in{\mathbb R}$ satisfy
    \[
        \nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in \bar \partial u(x).
    \]
    Then the subgradient inequality implies that
    \[
        u(z)\geq u(x)+(\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+a\nu(x))\cdot (z-x)\quad\text{for all }z\in \overline{\Omega}.
    \]
    Since $\Omega\in C^1$ and $\nu(x)$ is outward pointing, there exists a small $r>0$ such that $z=x-r\nu(x)\in \Omega$. Choosing this vector in the subgradient inequality and rearranging terms we get
    \[
        a\geq \frac{u(x)-u(x-r\nu(x))}{r},
    \]
    because $\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot\nu(x)=0$. The right-hand side is a finite real number and the estimate holds for every admissible $a$. Taking the infimum over all such $a$ yields
\[
q_u(x)\geq
\frac{u(x)-u(x-r\nu(x))}{r}>-\infty.
\]
Thus $q_u(x)\in(-\infty,\infty]$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:ray}
 Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex domain with $C^1$ boundary, let $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$, and suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. For every $x\in\partial\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ray}
 \bar{\partial} u(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 \{\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x):a\geq q_u(x)\},
     &q_u(x)<+\infty,\\
 \varnothing,&q_u(x)=+\infty.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular, if $q_u(x)<\infty$, the infimum in
\eqref{eq:defq} is attained.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We first show that every subgradient $p$ of $u$ at $x\in \partial\Omega$ can be decomposed as 
 \begin{equation}
 \label{eq:decomp-subgrad}
    p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\quad\text{for some }a\in{\mathbb R}.
 \end{equation}
 We argue similarly as in the second half of the proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:bdry-subdifferential} \ref{C1-functions}. Fix $p\in\bar\partial u(x)$, $\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$ and let $\gamma\colon (-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\partial\Omega$ be a $C^1$ boundary curve with $\gamma(0)=x$ and $\gamma'(0)=\tau_0$. The subgradient inequality with $\varphi=u|_{\partial\Omega}$ and $y=\gamma(t)$ gives
\[
 \varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(x)
 \geq p\cdot(\gamma(t)-x),\quad \text{for all }|t|<\varepsilon.
\]
Dividing by positive and negative $t$ and taking the limit $t\downarrow 0$ ($t\uparrow 0$) shows
\[
 p\cdot\tau_0=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot\tau_0.
\]
Since $\tau_0$ is arbitrary, every element of $\bar{\partial} u(x)$ has the form $\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)$ for some $a\in{\mathbb R}$. This establishes \eqref{eq:decomp-subgrad}.

Recall that the convexity of $\Omega$ implies
\[
    \nu(x)\cdot(z-x)\leq 0\quad\text{for all }z\in \overline{\Omega}
\]
and hence, if $p\in\bar \partial u(x)$ and $s\geq0$, then the subgradient inequality shows that
\[
 p+s\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x).
\]
Consequently, since $\bar \partial u(x)$ is always a closed convex set and every subgradient $p$ can be written in the form $p=\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+a\nu(x)$ (see~\eqref{eq:decomp-subgrad}), the possible normal components $a=p\cdot \nu(x)$ form either the empty set, a closed upper ray $[a_0,\infty)$, or all of ${\mathbb R}$.

Finally, we show that the last possibility cannot occur. To this end, fix a sufficiently small $r>0$ such that $z=x-r\nu(x)\in\Omega$. If $p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x)$ for some $a\in{\mathbb R}$, then the subgradient inequality at $z$ gives
\[
 u(z)\geq u(x)-ra,
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 a\geq\frac{u(x)-u(z)}{r}.
\]
Thus the normal components of the subgradients $p\in \bar\partial u(x)$ are bounded below whenever they are nonempty. By definition of $q_u$ (see~\eqref{eq:defq}), the closed-ray description is exactly \eqref{eq:ray}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:measurable}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex domain with $C^1$ boundary, let $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$, and suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. Then $q_u\colon \partial\Omega\to(-\infty,\infty]$ is Borel measurable.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
First note that by Corollary \ref{cor:supporting-hyperplanes} strict convexity of $\Omega$ is equivalent to the condition that for each $x\in \partial\Omega$ one has
 \begin{equation}
 \label{eq:equiv-cond-strict-proof}
        -\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)>0\quad\text{for all }z\in \overline{\Omega}\setminus \{x\}.
 \end{equation}
We claim that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:q-sup}
 q_u(x)=\sup_{z\in\Omega}
 \frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot(z-x)-u(z)}
 {-\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)},\quad \text{for each }x\in\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
If $q_u(x)<\infty$, then Lemma~\ref{lem:ray} implies that 
\[
    u(z)\geq u(x)+(\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+a\nu(x))\cdot (z-x)\quad\text{for all }z\in \overline{\Omega}
\]
for every $a\geq q_u(x)$. Equivalently,
\[
    -a\nu(x)\cdot (z-x)\geq u(x)+\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)\cdot (z-x)-u(z),\quad \text{for all }z\in \overline{\Omega}.
\]
Now, we choose $a=q_u(x)$ and use the positivity condition \eqref{eq:equiv-cond-strict-proof} to obtain
\[
     q_u(x)\geq \frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)\cdot (z-x)-u(z)}{-\nu(x)\cdot (z-x)},\quad \text{for all }z\in \Omega.
\]
Hence 
\[
q_u(x)\geq \sup_{z\in\Omega} \frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)\cdot (z-x)-u(z)}{-\nu(x)\cdot (z-x)}.
\]
Conversely, set
\[
S(x):=
\sup_{z\in\Omega}
\frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot(z-x)-u(z)}
{-\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)}.
\]
By the inequality $q_u(x)\geq S(x)$ and $q_u(x)<\infty$, we have $S(x)<\infty$. Moreover, the condition $u\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ implies $S(x)>-\infty$. Hence, by the definition of
$S(x)$,
\[
u(z)\geq
u(x)+\bigl(\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+S(x)\nu(x)\bigr)\cdot(z-x)
\qquad\text{for every }z\in\Omega.
\]
Since $u\in C(\overline{\Omega})$, this inequality also holds for every $z\in\overline\Omega$. Since $\bar u=+\infty$ outside $\overline\Omega$, it follows that
\[
\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+S(x)\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x).
\]
Hence Lemma~\ref{lem:ray} gives $q_u(x)\leq S(x)$. Hence, we have verified \eqref{eq:q-sup}, when $q_u(x)<\infty$.

Next, let us consider the case $q_u(x)=\infty$. We need to show $S(x)=+\infty$. If $S(x)<\infty$, then we again have $\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+S(x)\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x)$, but this contradicts \eqref{eq:ray}. Thus, $S(x)<\infty$ cannot hold. Hence also in this case \eqref{eq:q-sup} holds true.

To prove measurability, choose a countable dense subset
$D\subset\Omega$. For fixed $x\in\partial\Omega$, the function
\[
z\mapsto
\frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot(z-x)-u(z)}
{-\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)}
\]
is continuous on $\Omega$. Therefore,
\[
q_u(x)
=
\sup_{z\in D}
\frac{u(x)+\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot(z-x)-u(z)}
{-\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)}.
\]
For every fixed $z\in D$, the function
\[
x\mapsto
\frac{\varphi(x)+\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot(z-x)-u(z)}
{-\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)}
\]
is continuous on $\partial\Omega$, because the denominator is strictly positive by \eqref{eq:equiv-cond-strict-proof}. Consequently, $q_u$ is the supremum of countably many continuous functions and is therefore Borel measurable.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:gen-cauchy-data}
    Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex domain with $C^1$ boundary. For every finite Borel measure $\nu$, we define the \emph{generalized global Cauchy data set} by
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:generalized-global-Cauchy-data}
        \mathcal{C}^{\rm glob}_{\nu}\vcentcolon = \left\{(\varphi,q_{u_{\nu,\varphi}}):\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)\right\}.
    \end{equation}
    Here, $u_{\nu,\varphi}\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ is the Alexandrov solution of the Dirichlet problem 
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:MA-cauchy}
        \begin{cases}
            \det D^2 u=\nu,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
            u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega,
        \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    and $q_{u_{\nu,\varphi}}$ is the extremal boundary slope. The Cauchy data set is well-defined and is a subset of $C^1(\partial\Omega)\times\mathcal{B}(\partial\Omega)$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We first argue that all objects in \eqref{eq:generalized-global-Cauchy-data} are well-defined. First, for any finite Borel measure $\nu$ and every boundary datum $\varphi\in C(\partial\Omega)$, the Dirichlet problem \eqref{eq:MA-cauchy} is well-posed by Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-existence}. We will denote this unique Alexandrov solution by $u_{\nu,\varphi}\in C(\overline{\Omega})$. Second, Proposition~\ref{def:slope} shows that the boundary slope
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:slope-proof}
        \partial\Omega\ni x\mapsto q_{u_{\nu,\varphi}}(x)\in (-\infty,\infty]
    \end{equation}
    is well-defined, when $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$. Hence, all objects in \eqref{eq:generalized-global-Cauchy-data} are well-defined. Moreover, Proposition~\ref{lem:measurable} ensures that the map \eqref{eq:slope-proof} is Borel measurable, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex domain with $C^1$ boundary. For any nonnegative $f\in L^1(\Omega)$, we set
    \[
        \mathcal{C}^{\rm glob}_{f}\vcentcolon = \mathcal{C}^{\rm glob}_{f\,dx},
    \]
    where $\mathcal{C}^{\rm glob}_{f\,dx}$ is the generalized Cauchy data set provided by Proposition~\ref{def:gen-cauchy-data}.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded convex $C^1$ domain. Assume that $f\in C(\Omega)$ is nonnegative. We say that a tuple $(\varphi,\psi)\in C(\partial\Omega)\times C(\partial\Omega)$ is a \emph{classical Cauchy data pair} for the Monge--Ampère equation with source $f$ if $\varphi \in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$ and $\psi=\partial_\nu u|_{\partial\Omega}$, where $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$ is a convex pointwise solution of
     \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:classical-Cauchy}
    \begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega.
    \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:classical-compatibility}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded convex domain with $C^1$ boundary. Let $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$ and suppose that $f\in L^1(\Omega)\cap C(\Omega)$ is nonnegative. Assume that $u\in C^1(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and $\varphi=u|_{\partial\Omega}$. Then, for every $x\in\partial\Omega$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:classical-ray}
 q_u(x)=\partial_\nu u(x).
\end{equation}
If, in addition, $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$ and $u$ is an Alexandrov solution of the Monge--Ampère problem 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:classical-measure}
\begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
 u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
satisfying $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$, then the generalized and classical Cauchy pairs coincide.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Since $u\in C^1(\overline{\Omega})$, Lemma~\ref{lemma:bdry-subdifferential} implies
    \[
        \bar{\partial}u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)+s\nu(x):s\geq 0\},\quad x\in\partial\Omega.
    \]
    Hence the decomposition $\nabla u(x)=\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+(\partial_\nu u(x))\nu(x)$ and \eqref{eq:defq} show formula \eqref{eq:classical-ray}. 

    If, in addition, the Alexandrov solution $u$ belongs to $C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$, then Remark~\ref{rem:Alexandrov-Classica-sol} yields that $u$ is a classical solution of \eqref{eq:classical-measure}. Using the identity \eqref{eq:classical-ray}, we conclude that a generalized Cauchy pair is also a classical Cauchy pair. Conversely, by Remark~\ref{rem:Alexandrov-Classica-sol} every classical solution $u\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})\cap C^2(\Omega)$ is an Alexandrov solution and hence, once again, \eqref{eq:classical-ray} implies that every classical Cauchy pair is a generalized Cauchy pair.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:Interior-cauchy}
    Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain (see Definition~\ref{def:uniformly-convex-domains}). Assume that $f\in L^\infty (\Omega)$ is nonnegative. We say that a tuple $(\varphi,\psi)\in C(\partial\Omega)\times \mathcal{B}(\partial\Omega)$ is an \emph{interior Cauchy data pair} for the Monge--Ampère equation with source $f$ if $\varphi \in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$ and 
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:interior-normal}
        \psi(x)=\partial^{-}_\nu u(x)\vcentcolon =\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{u(x)-u(x-r\nu(x))}{r},\quad x\in\partial\Omega,
    \end{equation}
    where $u\in C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})$ is an Alexandrov solution of
     \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:interior-Cauchy}
    \begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega.
    \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{rem:one-sided-der}
For any proper convex function
$g:\mathbb R^n\to(-\infty,\infty]$, one may define the \emph{one-sided directional derivative} of $g$ at $x$ into direction $y$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:one-sided-limit}
g'(x;y)
\vcentcolon=
\lim_{\lambda\downarrow0}
\frac{g(x+\lambda y)-g(x)}{\lambda}\in [-\infty,\infty].
\end{equation}
By \cite[Theorem~23.1]{Rockafellar}, this limit exists at any $x$, where $g$ is finite. Indeed, the difference quotient is a nondeceasing function of $\lambda>0$, so that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:one-sided-der-inf}
    g'(x;y)=\inf_{\lambda>0}\frac{g(x+\lambda y)-g(x)}{\lambda}.
\end{equation}
Now, let $\bar u:\mathbb R^n\to(-\infty,\infty]$ be the canonical lower-semicontinuous convex extension of a convex function $u\colon \overline{\Omega}\to {\mathbb R}$ (see~\eqref{eq:extension-convex}). If $\Omega$ is a $C^1$ domain, then we have $x-r\nu(x)\in\Omega$ for all sufficiently small $r>0$ and hence
\[
\bar u'(x;-\nu(x))
=
\lim_{r\downarrow0}
\frac{\bar u(x-r\nu(x))-\bar u(x)}{r}
=
-\partial_\nu^-u(x).
\]
Thus $-\partial_\nu^-u(x)$ is precisely the one-sided directional derivative of $\bar u$ at $x$ in the inward direction $-\nu(x)$. Since $u\in C(\Omega)$ and the terms in the infimum are real-valued an nondecreasing, it follows that $\bar u'(x;-\nu(x))\in [-\infty,\infty)$ and hence $\partial_\nu^-u(x)\in (-\infty,\infty]$.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:radial-slope}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^1$ domain. Assume that $u\in C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega})$ is a convex function and suppose that
\[
    u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi
    \qquad\text{with }\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega).
\]
    Then, for every $x\in\partial\Omega$, the boundary subdifferential $\bar \partial u(x)$ is nonempty and the \emph{interior normal derivative} of $u$ at $x$ is given by
    \begin{equation}
\label{eq:radial-slope}
    \partial^{-}_\nu u(x)\vcentcolon =
\lim_{r\downarrow0}
\frac{u(x)-u(x-r\nu(x))}{r}=q_u(x).
\end{equation}
    Moreover, the limit in \eqref{eq:radial-slope} is finite and there holds
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bound-interior-normal}
|\partial^{-}_\nu u (x)|
\leq
    [u]_{C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega})}
\qquad
\text{for every }x\in\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
    In particular, generalized and interior Cauchy data pairs coincide, whenever the unique Alexandrov solution is Lipschitz on $\overline{\Omega}$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix $x\in\partial\Omega$. Since $\Omega$ is of class $C^1$ and $\nu$ is the outward pointing normal field, we have 
\[
x-r\nu(x)\in\Omega
\qquad
\text{for }0<r<r_0,
\]
when $r_0>0$ is sufficiently small. Define 
\[
g\colon [0,r_0)\to{\mathbb R},\quad g(r):=u(x-r\nu(x)).
\]
Since $u$ is convex, $g$ is a convex function of one variable on $[0,r_0)$. In particular, the difference quotient $r\mapsto \frac{g(r)-g(0)}{r}$ is monotonically nondecreasing on $(0,r_0)$. Indeed, if $0<r_1<r_2<r_0$, then
\[
    g(r_1)=g(r_2 t)=g((1-t)0+tr_2)\leq (1-t)g(0)+tg(r_2),
\]
where we set $t=r_1/r_2\in (0,1)$. Thus, $g(0)=u(x)\in {\mathbb R}$ implies
\[
    g(r_1)-g(0)\leq t(g(r_2)-g(0))=\frac{r_1}{r_2}(g(r_2)-g(0)).
\]
This establishes the claim.

Now, since the difference quotient of $g$ is nondecreasing on $(0,r_0)$, its right-sided derivative $g'_+(0)$ exists and is given by
\[
    g'_+(0)\vcentcolon = \lim_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{g(r)-g(0)}{r}=\inf_{0<r<r_0}\frac{g(r)-g(0)}{r}\in [-\infty,\infty).
\]
Since $u$ is Lipschitz on $\overline{\Omega}$, the function $g$ is Lipschitz on $[0,r_0]$ and hence the right-sided derivative $g'_+(0)$ is finite with
\[
|g'_+ (0)|\leq [g]_{C^{0,1}([0,r_0])}\leq [u]_{C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega})}.
\]
By definition of $g$, we conclude that $\partial^{-}_{\nu}u(x)$ exists, it is given by $-g'_+ (0)$, and the estimate \eqref{eq:bound-interior-normal} holds.

We next show that $\partial^{-}_{\nu}u(x)=q_u(x)$. Let
\[
    p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x).
\]
Applying the subgradient inequality at $x-r\nu(x)$ gives
\[
u(x-r\nu(x))
\geq
u(x)-ra.
\]
Hence
\[
\frac{u(x)-u(x-r\nu(x))}{r}\leq a.
\]
Passing to the limit as $r\downarrow0$ and then taking the infimum over all admissible $a$ yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:ell-lower-q}
    \partial^{-}_{\nu}u(x)\leq q_u(x);
\end{equation}
see Proposition~\ref{def:slope}.
If there is no such subgradient $p$, then $q_u(x)=\infty$ and \eqref{eq:ell-lower-q} holds trivially.

It remains to prove the reverse inequality and, at the same time, the nonemptiness of $\bar\partial u(x)$. Let $r_k\downarrow0$ and set
\[
x_k:=x-r_k\nu(x)\in\Omega.
\]
Since $u\in C(\Omega)$ and $x_k\in \Omega$, we may choose $p_k\in\partial u(x_k)$. By the subgradient inequality and the Lipschitz continuity of $u$, we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:boundedness-p-lipschitz}
|p_k|\leq [u]_{C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega})}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, if $p_k\neq 0$, then apply the subgradient inequality at $x_k$ with $z\vcentcolon =x_k+r\frac{p_k}{|p_k|}$, where $r>0$ is chosen sufficiently small such that $z\in\Omega$. We have
\[
    u(x_k+r p_k/|p_k|)-u(x_k)\geq r|p_k|.
\]
Hence, the Lipschitz continuity of $u$ yields \eqref{eq:boundedness-p-lipschitz}.

Therefore after passing to a subsequence, we may assume that $p_k\to p\in\mathbb R^n$. Since $u=\bar u$ in $\overline{\Omega}$ and $u$ is Lipschitz continuous up to the boundary, the subgradient inequality
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subgrad-ineq-interior}
u(z)\geq u(x_k)+p_k\cdot(z-x_k)
\end{equation}
holds for all $z\in \overline{\Omega}$ and thus passing to the limit $k\to\infty$ shows that $p\in\bar\partial u(x)$. Thus, $\bar\partial u(x)$ is indeed nonempty.

Moreover, restriction of the preceding supporting inequality to the normal segment $z=x-t\nu(x)\in \Omega$, $t>0$, gives
\[
    g(t)\geq g(r_k)-p_k\cdot \nu(x)(t-r_k).
\]
Thus, $-p_k\cdot \nu(x)\in \partial g(r_k)$. For a one-dimensional convex function, if $r_k\downarrow 0$ and $\xi_k\in \partial g(r_k)$, then $\xi_k\to g'_+(0)$. Indeed, the subgradient inequality at $r_k$ with $t=0$, $t=s>0$, and subgradient $\xi_k$ gives
\[
    \frac{g(r_k)-g(0)}{r_k}\leq \xi_k\leq \frac{g(s)-g(r_k)}{s-r_k}.
\]
Hence, by $g\in C([0,r_0))$, passing to the limit $k\to\infty$ and then $s\downarrow 0$ yields $\xi_k\to g'_+(0)$.

Therefore, $p_k\to p$ yields
\[
