-p\cdot\nu(x)=g'_+(0),
\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
p\cdot\nu(x)=\partial^-_\nu u(x).
\]
Applying the subgradient inequality
\[
    u(z)\geq u(x)+p\cdot (z-x),\quad z\in \overline{\Omega}
\]
with $z=\gamma(t)$, where $\gamma\colon (-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to \partial \Omega$ is a $C^1$ boundary curves with $\gamma(0)=x$ and $\gamma'(0)=\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$, we get
\[
    p\cdot \tau_0=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)\cdot \tau_0.
\]
Since this holds for all tangential vectors $\tau_0$, we find
\[
p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in \bar \partial u(x)
\]
for some $a\in{\mathbb R}$. Since $p\cdot \nu(x)=\partial^-_\nu u(x)$, we have 
\[
    p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+(\partial^-_\nu u(x))\nu(x)\in \bar \partial u(x).
\]
From the definition of  $q_u$ (see~\eqref{eq:defq}), we may conclude that 
\[
q_u(x)\leq \partial^-_\nu u(x).
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:ell-lower-q}, this proves \eqref{eq:radial-slope}.

Moreover, using $p\cdot\nu(x)=\partial^-_\nu u(x)$, $p_k\to p$, and \eqref{eq:boundedness-p-lipschitz} we may calculate
\[
    |\partial^-_\nu u(x)|=|p\cdot \nu(x)|=\lim_{k\to\infty}|p_k\cdot \nu(x)|\leq [u]_{C^{0,1}(\overline{\Omega})},
\]
which establishes \eqref{eq:bound-interior-normal}.

The last part of the lemma is a direct consequence of \eqref{eq:radial-slope} and Definition~\ref{def:Interior-cauchy}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:boundary-Lipschitz}
Let $\Omega \subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain, let $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$, and suppose that $f\in L^\infty (\Omega)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:upper-bound-f}
 0\leq f\leq \lambda\quad\text{a.e. in }\Omega.
\end{equation}
Then the unique Alexandrov solution $u$ of 
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:bdry-Lipschitz}
    \begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega,
    \end{cases}
    \end{equation}
    provided by Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-existence}, is Lipschitz on $\overline\Omega$. Moreover, its extremal boundary slope $q_u\colon \partial\Omega\to{\mathbb R}$ is a bounded Borel measurable function.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Since $\Omega$ is a bounded uniformly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain, Lemma~\ref{lem:defining-function} implies that there exist $\rho\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb R^n)$ and $c_0>0$ such that
\[
\Omega=\{\rho<0\},\qquad
\partial\Omega=\{\rho=0\},\qquad
D^2\rho\geq c_0I
\quad\text{a.e. in }\mathbb R^n.
\]
Using boundary flattening charts and a finite partition of unity, we may construct an extension $\widetilde\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb R^n)$ of $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$ (see, e.g., \cite[p.~94]{GT}). Since $\Omega$ is bounded, \cite[Section~4.2.3]{evans-mt} implies that $\widetilde \varphi \in W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)$. By Rademacher's theorem the almost-everywhere Hessian $D^2 \widetilde \varphi$ represents its weak Hessian. Therefore, we may estimate
\[
D^2\widetilde\varphi\geq -M_\varphi I
\quad\text{a.e. in }\Omega,
\]
where we set $M_\varphi\vcentcolon = \|D^2 \widetilde \varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}$. Set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lambda0}
\lambda_0:=\max\{1,\lambda^{1/n}\}
\end{equation}
and choose $A>0$ so that
\[
Ac_0-M_\varphi\geq\lambda_0.
\]
Then
\[
w:=\widetilde\varphi+A\rho
\]
satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:properties-w-lipschitz}
w=\varphi\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega,
\qquad
D^2w\geq\lambda_0I
\quad\text{a.e. in }\Omega.
\end{equation}
In particular, $w$ is strongly convex, that is, 
\[
    w(y)\geq w(x)+\nabla w(x)\cdot (y-x)+\frac{\lambda_0}{2}|x-y|^2
\]
for all $x,y\in \Omega$. If $w$ is of class $C^2$ this is a classical result and in our more general setup it can be justified by mollification. This argument is carried out in detail at the beginning of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:defining-function}.

By Lemma~\ref{lem:MA-measure} \ref{C11-case}, the Monge--Amp\`ere measure of $w$ is $\mu_w=\det D^2w\,dx$. Moreover, \eqref{eq:properties-w-lipschitz}, monotonicity of the determinant, \eqref{eq:lambda0}, \eqref{eq:upper-bound-f}, and the fact that $u$ is the Alexandrov solution of \eqref{eq:bdry-Lipschitz} imply
\[
\mu_w=\det D^2w\,dx
\geq\lambda_0^n\,dx
\geq\lambda\,dx
\geq f\,dx=\mu_u
\]
as Borel measures. Because $w=\varphi=u$ on $\partial\Omega$, the comparison
principle (Proposition~\ref{prop:Comparison}) gives
\[
w\leq u\quad\text{in }\Omega.
\]

Fix $x\in\partial\Omega$. Every relative supporting plane of $w$
at $x$ also supports $u$ there, since $w\leq u$ in
$\overline\Omega$ and $w(x)=u(x)$. Hence
\[
\bar\partial w(x)\subset \bar\partial u(x)
\]
and therefore \eqref{eq:defq} yields
\[
q_u(x)\leq q_w(x).
\]
Since $w\in C^{1,1}(\overline{\Omega})$ is convex, Proposition~\ref{prop:classical-compatibility} demonstrates that
\[
q_w(x)=\partial_\nu w(x)\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega).
\]
Thus $q_w$, and hence $q_u$ from above, is uniformly bounded on $\partial\Omega$.

The $C^{1,1}$ tubular-neighborhood theorem and compactness of $\partial\Omega$ give $r_0>0$ such that
\[
    z_x:=x-r_0\nu(x)\in\Omega \qquad\text{for every }x\in\partial\Omega.
\]
Let
\[
p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\in\bar \partial u(x).
\]
The supporting inequality at $z_x$ gives
\[
u(z_x)\geq\varphi(x)-r_0a,
\]
and hence
\[
a\geq\frac{\varphi(x)-u(z_x)}{r_0}.
\]
Since a convex function on a compact convex set cannot exceed the
maximum of its boundary values,
\[
u(z_x)\leq\max_{\partial\Omega}\varphi.
\]
It follows that
\[
a\geq
\frac{\min_{\partial\Omega}\varphi
      -\max_{\partial\Omega}\varphi}{r_0}.
\]
Recalling the definition \eqref{eq:defq}, we see that taking the infimum over all admissible $a$ gives a uniform lower bound for $q_u$, namely,
\[
    q_u(x)\geq \frac{\min_{\partial\Omega}\varphi
      -\max_{\partial\Omega}\varphi}{r_0}.
\]
We have therefore proved $q_u\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)$ with
\[
\sup_{x\in\partial\Omega}|q_u(x)|\leq \max\left(\frac{\max_{\partial\Omega}\varphi-\min_{\partial\Omega}\varphi}{r_0},\|\partial_\nu w\
\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}\right).
\]
Since Lemma~\ref{lem:measurable} guarantees the Borel measurability of $q_u$, we have $q_u\in \mathcal{B}_b(\partial\Omega)$.

It remains to prove the global Lipschitz estimate. By Lemma~\ref{lem:ray}, the infimum defining $q_u(x)$ is attained. Thus
\[
p_x:=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+q_u(x)\nu(x)
\in\bar\partial u(x),
\qquad x\in\partial\Omega.
\]
Since $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$, $q_u\in L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)$, and $\nu(x)\in \mathbb S^{n-1}$, there is $M>0$ such that
\[
|p_x|\leq M
\qquad\text{for every }x\in\partial\Omega.
\]
Now, let $x,y\in \overline{\Omega}$,
$x\neq y$, and let the line through $x$ and $y$ intersect $\overline{\Omega}$ in the chord $[a,b]$ for some boundary points $a,b\in \partial\Omega$. Let us set
\[
    \ell\vcentcolon = |b-a|,\quad  e\vcentcolon =\frac{b-a}{\ell}\in \mathbb S^{n-1}
\]
and define the function
\[
    g\colon [0,\ell]\to{\mathbb R},\quad g(s):=u(a+se).
\]
Since $u\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ is convex, the function $g$ is convex as well. Convexity of $g$ and the subgradient inequalities of $u$ associated with $p_a$ and $p_b$ imply
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:two-sided-bound}
p_a\cdot e
\leq\frac{g(t)-g(s)}{t-s}
\leq p_b\cdot e
\qquad
(0\leq s<t\leq|b-a|).
\end{equation}
Indeed, using the subgradient inequality and $b=a+\ell \, e$, we get
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subgrad-1}
    g(t)=u(a+te)\geq u(a)+t\, p_a\cdot e=g(0)+t\,p_a\cdot e
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subgrad-2}
    g(s)=u(a+s e)\geq u(b)+(s-\ell)\,p_b \cdot e=g(\ell)+(s-\ell)\,p_b \cdot e.
\end{equation}
Moreover, the convexity gives
\[
\begin{split}
     g(s)&=g\left((1-s/t)0+(s/t)t\right)\\
     &\leq (1-s/t)g(0)+(s/t)g(t)\\
     &=(1-s/t)(g(0)-g(t))+g(t)
\end{split}
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cons-conv}
    \frac{g(t)-g(s)}{t-s}\geq \frac{g(t)-g(0)}{t}.
\end{equation}
Combined with \eqref{eq:subgrad-1}, we get
\[
    \frac{g(t)-g(s)}{t-s}\geq \frac{g(t)-g(0)}{t}\geq p_a\cdot e.
\]
Similarly, since
\[
    t=\frac{\ell-t}{\ell-s}s+\frac{t-s}{\ell-s}\ell,
\]
convexity of $g$ implies
\[
    g(t)\leq \frac{\ell-t}{\ell-s}g(s)+\frac{t-s}{\ell-s}g(\ell).
\]
Therefore,
\[
   \frac{g(t)-g(s)}{t-s}\leq \frac{g(\ell)-g(s)}{\ell-s}\leq p_b\cdot e. 
\]
This completes the verification of \eqref{eq:two-sided-bound}.

Since $|p_a|,|p_b|\leq M$ and $e\in \mathbb S^{n-1}$, we obtain
\[
|g(t)-g(s)|\leq M|t-s|.
\]
Applying this to the parameters corresponding to $x$ and $y$
gives
\[
|u(x)-u(y)|\leq M|x-y|.
\]
Thus $u\in C^{0,1}(\overline\Omega)$, and the proposition follows.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:DiNmap}
    Let $\Omega$ be uniformly convex with $C^{1,1}$ boundary and suppose that $f\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)$ satisfies $0\leq f\leq C$. Then, the \emph{global Dirichlet-to-interior-Neumann map} $\Lambda_f^-
\colon
C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)
\to
\mathcal B_b(\partial\Omega)$, given by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-DiN-one-sided-slope}
\Lambda_f^-(\varphi)
\vcentcolon = \partial_\nu^-u_{f,\varphi}\vcentcolon =\lim_{r\downarrow0}
\frac{u_{f,\varphi}(x)-u_{f,\varphi}(x-r\nu(x))}{r},
\end{equation}
is well-defined. Moreover, $\Lambda_f^-$ is completely determined by the generalized Cauchy data set $\mathcal{C}^{\rm glob}_f$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
First, the Alexandrov solution $u_{f,\varphi}$ is well-defined by Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-existence}. Then Proposition~\ref{prop:boundary-Lipschitz}  yields that $u_{f,\varphi}$ is Lipschitz continuous up to the boundary. Therefore, we can apply Lemma~\ref{lem:radial-slope} to deduce that the right hand side of \eqref{eq:def-DiN-one-sided-slope} is well-defined and coincides with the extremal boundary slope $q_{u_{f,\varphi}}$. Combined with Lemma~\ref{lem:measurable}, this shows that $\partial_\nu^-u_{f,\varphi}$ is a bounded Borel measurable function.
    
    
    The last part of the assertion follows from the formulas \eqref{eq:radial-slope} and \eqref{eq:def-DiN-one-sided-slope}. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{def:subdiff-image}
    Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded convex $C^1$ domain, let $\varphi\in C^1(\partial\Omega)$, and suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. Moreover, let $q_u$ be its extremal boundary slope as defined in Proposition~\ref{def:slope}. Then we denote the \emph{subdifferential image} by
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:subdiff-image}
         \mathrm D_u\vcentcolon =\partial u(\Omega)\subset{\mathbb R}^n,
    \end{equation}
    the \emph{boundary subdifferential image} by 
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:bdry-subdiff-image}
         \mathrm B_u\vcentcolon =\bar\partial u(\partial \Omega)\subset{\mathbb R}^n,
    \end{equation}
    and the \emph{boundary slope image} by
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:S}
     \mathrm S_u\vcentcolon =\{\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+q_u(x)\nu(x):
             x\in\partial\Omega,\ q_u(x)<\infty\}\subset \mathrm B_u.
    \end{equation}
    Moreover, for every $\psi\in C(\partial\Omega)$ and $p\in {\mathbb R}^n$, we denote by 
    \[
         \mathcal{X}_\psi (p)\vcentcolon = \operatorname*{arg\,max}_{x\in\partial\Omega}
     \bigl(p\cdot x-\psi(x)\bigr)\subset \partial\Omega
    \]
    the set of \emph{boundary Legendre maximizers}.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
We note that the continuiuty of $\psi$ and the compactness of $\partial\Omega$ imply that $\mathcal{X}_\psi (p)$ is always nonempty and compact.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set and assume that $u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R}$ is convex. Let $\mathrm d_u$ denote the set of differentiability points of $u$. Then $D_u=\partial u(\Omega)$ is contained in the closed convex hull of $\nabla u(\mathrm d_u)$.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:S-null}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$. Suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. Then
\[
  \mathcal L^n(\mathrm S_u)=0.
\]
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The result follows by standard measure theory arguments. Recall that by Lemma~\ref{lem:measurable} the extremal boundary slope $q_u\colon \partial\Omega\to (-\infty,\infty]$ is Borel measurable. Let us denote the graph of $q_u$ by
\[
    \Gamma\vcentcolon = \operatorname{graph}(q_u)=\{(x,q_u(x)):x\in \partial\Omega\}\subset \partial\Omega\times (-\infty,\infty].
\]
For each $k\in\mathbb N$, define the Borel sets
\[
 E_k\vcentcolon =\{x\in\partial\Omega:|q_u(x)|\leq k\}\subset{\mathbb R}^n,
\]
which cover $\{q_u<\infty\}$ as $k$ runs through the whole natural numbers. Moreover, we write $\Gamma_k$ for the restriction of $\Gamma$ to $E_k$, that is, it is the graph of the Borel function $q_u^k\vcentcolon =q_u|_{E_k}$. More explicitly,
\[
 \Gamma_k\vcentcolon = \operatorname{graph}(q_u^k)=\{(x,q_u(x)):x\in E_k\}
       \subset\partial\Omega\times [-k,k]\subset {\mathbb R}^{n+1}.
\]
Since $q_u^k\colon E_k\to {\mathbb R}$ is Borel measurable, the sets $\Gamma_k$ are Borel as well. For any $k\in{\mathbb N}$, we define the Lipschitz continuous function 
\[
    \Psi_k\colon \partial\Omega\times [-k,k]\to {\mathbb R}^n,\quad \Psi_k(x,a)\vcentcolon = \nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+a\nu(x).
\]
Note that Lipschitz continuity of $\Psi_k$ follows from the fact that $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$ and $\partial\Omega\in C^{1,1}$, which implies $\nu\in C^{0,1}$. More precisely,
\[
    |\Psi_k(x,a)-\Psi_k(y,b)|\leq ([\nabla_\tau \varphi]_{C^{0,1}(\partial\Omega)}+k[\nu]_{C^{0,1}(\partial\Omega)})|x-y|+|a-b|.
\]
Then, we may decompose $\mathrm S_u$ as
\[
 \mathrm S_u=\bigcup_{k=1}^\infty\Psi_k(\Gamma_k).
\]
By $\sigma$-subadditivity, we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:subadditivity-estimate}
    \mathcal{L}^n(\mathrm S_u)\leq \sum_{k\in{\mathbb N}}\mathcal{L}^n(\Psi_k (\Gamma_k)).
\end{equation}
We claim that $\mathcal H^n(\Gamma_k)=0$. To see this, cover $\partial\Omega$ by finitely many $C^{1,1}$ boundary charts $X_j:V_j\subset\mathbb R^{n-1}\to\partial\Omega$, $1\leq j\leq N$. For each $1\leq j\leq N$ and $k\in{\mathbb N}$, set
\[
    V_{j,k}\vcentcolon = X_j^{-1}(E_k),\quad X_{j,k}\vcentcolon = X_j|_{V_{j,k}}
\]
and
\[
G_{j,k}
\vcentcolon =\operatorname{graph}(q_u^k\circ X_{j,k})=
\left\{
(z,a):
z\in V_{j,k},\ a=q_u(X_j(z))
\right\}\subset {\mathbb R}^n.
\]
Since $E_k$ is a Borel set, $V_{j,k}$ and $G_{j,k}$ are Borel too. The vertical section $(G_{j,k})_z$ is always a singleton, namely $\{q_u (X_j(z))\}$, and hence $\mathcal{L}^1((G_{j,k})_z)=0$ for every $z\in V_{j,k}$. Since $G_{j,k}$ is Borel, Tonelli's theorem yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L^n(G_{j,k})
&=
\int_{V_{j,k}}\int_{-k}^{k}
\chi_{G_{j,k}}(z,a)\,da\,dz\\
&=
\int_{V_{j,k}}\mathcal L^1((G_{j,k})_z)\,dz
=0.
\end{aligned}
\]
We have
\[
\begin{split}
     \Gamma_k&=\bigcup_{j=1}^N\{(x,q_u(x)):x\in E_k\cap X_j(V_j)\}\\
     &=\bigcup_{j=1}^N\{(X_j(z),q_u(X_j(z)):z\in X_j^{-1}(E_k)\cap V_j \}\\
     &=\bigcup_{j=1}^N\{(X_j(z),q_u(X_j(z)):z\in V_{j,k} \}\\
     &=\bigcup_{j=1}^N\Phi_j(G_{j,k}),
\end{split}
\]
where $\Phi_j\colon V_j\times {\mathbb R}\to{\mathbb R}^{n+1}$ is the Lipschitz map $\Phi_j(z,a)\vcentcolon = (X_j(z),a)$. Hence, the Lipschitz estimate for Hausdorff measure \cite[Section~2.4.1]{evans-mt} therefore implies 
\[
    \mathcal H^n(\Gamma_k)\leq \sum_{j=1}^N \mathcal{H}^n(\Phi_j(G_{j,k}))\leq \sum_{j=1}^N [\Phi_j]^n_{C^{0,1}(V_j\times {\mathbb R})}\mathcal{H}^n(G_{j,k})=0.
\]
Finally, since the maps $\Psi_k$ are Lipschitz continuous and $\mathcal{H}^n(\Gamma_k)=0$, the Lipschitz estimate for Hausdorff measure \cite[Section~2.4.1]{evans-mt} and $\mathcal{H}^n=\mathcal{L}^n$ on ${\mathbb R}^n$ imply that
\[
    \mathcal L^n(\Psi_k(\Gamma_k))\leq [\Psi_k]^n_{C^{0,1}(\partial\Omega\times [-k,k])}\mathcal{H}^n(\Gamma_k)=0.
\]
Thus, \eqref{eq:subadditivity-estimate} yields $\mathcal{L}^n(\mathrm S_u)=0$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
\label{def:Legendre-transform}
    Let $v\colon {\mathbb R}^n \to (-\infty,\infty]$ be a proper convex function. Then its \emph{Legendre transform} of $v$ (also called \emph{conjugate}) is the function $v^\star\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to (-\infty,\infty]$ defined by
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Legendre-trafo}
        v^\star (p)\vcentcolon = \sup_{x\in{\mathbb R}^n}[p\cdot x-v(x)],\quad p\in{\mathbb R}^n.
    \end{equation}
    Similarly, if $u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R}$ is a convex function over a convex open set $\Omega\subset {\mathbb R}^n$, then we define its Legendre transform $u^\star\colon {\mathbb R}^n \to (-\infty,\infty]$ as
    \[
        u^\star (p)\vcentcolon = \sup_{x\in\Omega}[p\cdot x-u(x)],\quad p\in{\mathbb R}^n.
    \]
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:legendre-transform}
    Let $v\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to (-\infty,\infty]$ be a proper convex function.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
        \item\label{Legendre-convex} The Legendre transform $v^\star\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to (-\infty,\infty]$ is a proper convex lower semicontinuous function. 
        \item\label{involution-legendre} The Legendre transform is an involution on the collection of proper convex lower semicontinuous functions, that is, $(v^\star)^\star=v$.
        \item\label{subddif-legendre} Assume that $v$ is lower semicontinuous. Then for all $x\in {\mathbb R}^n$ and $p\in{\mathbb R}^n$, the following conditions are equivalent:
        \begin{enumerate}[(a)]
            \item $p\in \partial v(x)$;
            \item $x\in \partial v^\star (p)$;
            \item $z\mapsto p\cdot z-v(z)$ achieves its supremum at $z=x$, or equivalently $v^\star (p)=p\cdot x-v(x)$;
            \item $q\mapsto q\cdot x-v^\star (q)$ achieves its supremum at $q=p$, or equivalently $(v^\star)^\star (x)=p\cdot x-v^\star (p)$;
            \item $v(x)+v^\star (p)=p\cdot x$.
        \end{enumerate}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
The properties \ref{Legendre-convex}--\ref{involution-legendre} are reformulations of \cite[Theorem~12.2]{Rockafellar}. The equivalent conditions \ref{subddif-legendre} can be found in \cite[Theorem~23.5]{Rockafellar}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-13: body_md -->
\label{prop:D-characterization}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$. Suppose that $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ is convex and satisfies $u|_{\partial\Omega}=\varphi$. For every $p\notin \mathrm S_u$, we have the following characterization:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:D-characterization}
 p\notin \mathrm D_u
 \,\Leftrightarrow\,
 \exists x\in \mathcal{X}_\varphi(p):\,
 q_u(x)<\infty\text{ and }p\cdot\nu(x)>q_u(x).
\end{equation}
Moreover, the pair $(\varphi,q_u)$ determines $\mathrm D_u$ up to a Lebesgue null set.
<!-- end proposition-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Suppose first that $x\in \mathcal{X}_\varphi(p)$ and
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pos-cond}
      q_u(x)<\infty \quad\text{and}\quad p\cdot\nu(x)>q_u(x).
    \end{equation}
By definition of the set $\mathcal{X}_\varphi (p)$, the boundary function $\Phi_p\vcentcolon \partial\Omega\to{\mathbb R}$.
$\Phi_p (y)\vcentcolon =p\cdot y-\varphi(y)$ has a maximum at $y=x$. Thus, let $\gamma\colon (-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to \partial\Omega$ be a $C^1$ curve with $\gamma(0)=x$ and $\gamma'(0)=\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$. Then $\Phi_p\circ \gamma\colon (-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to{\mathbb R}$ has a maximum at $t=0$ and therefore
\[
    0=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\Phi_p(\gamma(t))=(p-\nabla_{\tau}\varphi(x))\cdot \tau_0.
\]
Since $\tau_0\in T_x\partial\Omega$ is arbitrary, this yields
\[
    p=\nabla_\tau \varphi(x)+a\nu(x)\quad\text{for some }a\in{\mathbb R}.
\]
The condition \eqref{eq:pos-cond} implies $a>q_u(x)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:ray},
\[
 p_0\vcentcolon =\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+q_u(x)\nu(x) \in \bar \partial u(x).
\]
Hence, for every $z\in\Omega$,
\begin{align*}
 &[u(z)-p\cdot z]-[u(x)-p\cdot x]\\
 &\qquad\geq(p_0-p)\cdot(z-x)\\
 &\qquad=(q_u(x)-a)\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)>0.
\end{align*}
Here we used that $a>q_u(x)$ and $\nu(x)\cdot(z-x)<0$ for every $z\in\Omega$, which follows from the strict convexity assumption on $\Omega$. Hence, 
\[
    p\cdot x-u(x) > p\cdot z -u(z)\quad\text{for all }z\in \Omega
\]
and therefore the maximum of $\overline{\Omega}\ni z\mapsto p\cdot z-u(z)\in{\mathbb R}$ not achieved at an interior point. Thus, Proposition~\ref{prop:legendre-transform} \ref{subddif-legendre} guarantees that $p\notin \mathrm D_u$.

Conversely, suppose $p\notin \mathrm D_u$. Since $u\in C(\overline\Omega)$ and $\overline\Omega$ is compact,
the function $z\mapsto p\cdot z-u(z)$ attains its maximum. By Proposition~\ref{prop:legendre-transform}
\ref{subddif-legendre} and $p\notin\mathrm D_u$, no maximizer lies in $\Omega$. Hence we may choose a maximizer $x\in\partial\Omega$. Then $x\in \mathcal{X}_\varphi(p)$ and, by Proposition~\ref{prop:legendre-transform} \ref{subddif-legendre}, $p\in \bar \partial u(x)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:ray} gives $q_u(x)<\infty$ and
$p\cdot\nu(x)\geq q_u(x)$. Equality would imply
\[
 p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+q_u(x)\nu(x)\in \mathrm S_u,
\]
contrary to the assumption $p\notin \mathrm S_u$. Hence the inequality is strict.

Therefore we have established the equivalence \eqref{eq:D-characterization}.

Finally, we show that $(\varphi,q_u)$ determines $\mathrm D_u$ up to a Lebesgue null set. We may write
\[
    \mathrm D_u=(\mathrm D_u\cap \mathrm S_u)\cup (\mathrm D_u\setminus \mathrm S_u).
\]
The set $\mathrm D_u\cap \mathrm S_u$ has vanishing $n$-dimensional Lebesgue measure by Lemma~\ref{lem:S-null}. On the other hand, we have
\[
(\mathrm D_u\setminus \mathrm S_u)=\{p\in{\mathbb R}^n:\forall x\in \mathcal{X}_\varphi(p)\text{ one has }x\in \{q_u=\infty\}\text{ or }p\cdot \nu(x)< q_u(x)\}.
\]
Indeed, if $p\in\mathrm S_u$, then for some $x\in\partial\Omega$ with $q_u(x)<\infty$,
\[
p=\nabla_\tau\varphi(x)+q_u(x)\nu(x)\in\bar\partial u(x);
\]
see Lemma~\ref{lem:ray}. Consequently, $x\in\mathcal X_\varphi(p)$ and
$p\cdot\nu(x)=q_u(x)$, so $p$ does not belong to the
right-hand side of the above identity. 

Since the identity for $\mathrm D_u\setminus \mathrm S_u$ only depends on $(\varphi,q_u)$, we deduce that it determines $\mathrm D_u$ up to a Lebesgue null set.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:D-equality}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain and $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$. Let $u_1,u_2\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ be convex, have the same boundary value $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$, and satisfy
$q_{u_1}=q_{u_2}$ on $\partial\Omega$. Then\footnote{Recall that we denote by $A\mathbin\triangle B$ the symmetric difference between the sets $A\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ and $B\subset{\mathbb R}^n$. More concretely,
\[
    A\mathbin\triangle B=(A\setminus B)\cup (B\setminus A).
\]}
\[
 \mathcal L^n(\mathrm D_{u_1}\mathbin\triangle \mathrm D_{u_2})=0.
\]
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Since $u_1,u_2$ have the same boundary value $\varphi$ and $q_{u_1}=q_{u_2}$ on $\partial\Omega$, their boundary slope images coincide:
\[
\mathrm S_{u_1}=\mathrm S_{u_2}=:\mathrm S.
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:D-characterization} implies that, for every $p\notin\mathrm S$,
\[
p\in\mathrm D_{u_1}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
p\in\mathrm D_{u_2}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\mathrm D_{u_1}\mathbin\triangle\mathrm D_{u_2}
\subset\mathrm S.
\]
By Lemma~\ref{lem:S-null}, $\mathcal L^n(\mathrm S)=0$, which proves the assertion.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proposition-14: body_md -->
\label{prop:weighted}
    Let $\Omega\subset {\mathbb R}^n$ be an open convex set and let $f\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative. Suppose that the convex function $u\colon \Omega\to {\mathbb R}$ is an Alexandrov solution of the Monge--Ampère equation   
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:MA-integral}
        \det D^2 u=f\quad\text{in }\Omega.
    \end{equation}
Then, for every bounded Borel function $H\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to{\mathbb R}$, one has 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted}
     \int_\Omega H(\nabla u(x))f(x)\,dx =\int_{\mathrm D_u}H(p)\,dp.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-14 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
We note that the gradient in the left hand side of the change of variables formula \eqref{eq:weighted} may be defined arbitrarily on the nondifferentiability set of $u$, because convex functions are almost everywhere differentiable.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Since $u$ is a convex function, Lemma~\ref{lem:diff-convex} shows that $u$ is locally Lipschitz and hence, by Rademacher's theorem, $u$ is almost everywhere differentiable. Let us denote this exceptional set by
    \[
        N\vcentcolon = \{x\in\Omega : u\text{ is not differentiable in }x\}.
    \]
    Since $\mathcal{L}^n$ is Borel regular, there exists a Borel set $\mathcal{N}\subset\Omega$ such that $N\subset \mathcal{N}$ and $\mathcal{L}^n(\mathcal{N})=\mathcal{L}^n(N)=0$. Moreover, by definition of an Alexandrov solution to \eqref{eq:MA-integral}, we have
    \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:MA-change-variables}
        \mu_u=f\,dx\quad\text{as Borel measures}
    \end{equation}
   and hence
    \[
         \mu_u(\mathcal{N})=\int_{\mathcal{ N}} f\,dx=0.
    \]
By the definition of Alexandrov measure,
\[
 \mathcal L^n(\partial u(\mathcal{N}))=0.
\]

Let us define the Borel set $A\vcentcolon =\Omega\setminus \mathcal{N}$.  By Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad}, we have $\partial u(x)=\{\nabla u(x)\}$ for every $x\in A$. Therefore
\[
 \mathrm D_u=\nabla u(A)\cup\partial u(\mathcal{N}).
\]
We deduce that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:D-grad}
\begin{split}
     \mathcal L^n(\mathrm D_u\mathbin\triangle\nabla u(A))&=\mathcal{L}^n(D_u\setminus \nabla u(A))=\mathcal{L}^n(\partial u(\mathcal{N})\setminus \nabla u(A))=0.
\end{split}
\end{equation}

Moreover, for any Borel set $B\subset {\mathbb R}^n$, the preimage
\[
 E_B\vcentcolon =(\nabla u|_A)^{-1}(B)=\{x\in A:\nabla u(x)\in B\}
\]
is Borel, because $\nabla u\colon A\to{\mathbb R}^n$ is Borel measurable. The Borel measurability of $\nabla u|_A$ follows from the fact that the components of $\nabla u(x)$, $x\in A$, are the pointwise limit of continuous functions, which are by themselves Borel measurable (see \cite[Section~3.1.2]{evans-mt}). Also, $\mathrm D_u$ is Borel measurable. Indeed, let $(K_n)_{n\in{\mathbb N}}\subset \Omega$ be an exhaustion by compact sets, then 
\[
    \mathrm D_u=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb N}}\partial u(K_n).
\]
Since $\partial u(K_n)$ are compact by Lemma~\ref{lemma:compactness-subgradinets}, $\mathrm D_u$ is a Borel set. Then
\[
 \mu_u(E_B)=\mathcal L^n(\partial u(E_B))
 =\mathcal L^n(\nabla u(E_B)).
\]
Using that for every function $F\colon X\to Y$ between two sets $X$ and $Y$, one has the $F(F^{-1}(Y'))=Y'\cap F(X)$, we have $\nabla u(E_B)=B\cap \nabla u(A)$. Hence \eqref{eq:D-grad} yields
\[
 \mu_u(E_B)=\mathcal L^n(\nabla u(E_B))=\mathcal L^n(B\cap \nabla u(A))=\mathcal L^n(B\cap \mathrm D_u).
\]
Finally, let us define
\[
    \overline{\nabla u}(x)\vcentcolon =\begin{cases}
        \nabla u(x),\quad & x\in A,\\
        0,\quad & x\in \mathcal{N}=\Omega\setminus A,
    \end{cases}
\]
which is again a Borel measurable function. By
\eqref{eq:MA-change-variables} and $\mathcal{L}^n(\mathcal{N})=0$ we get
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:equal-as-borel}
 (\overline{\nabla u})_\#(f\,dx)=\mathcal L^n\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex} \mathrm D_u\text{ as Borel measures}.
\end{equation}

Now, the integral identity \eqref{eq:weighted} follows from \eqref{eq:equal-as-borel}, since $\overline{\nabla u}_{A}=\nabla u$ and $\mathcal{L}^n(\Omega\setminus A)=\mathcal{L}^n(\mathcal{N})=0$. Indeed, it first gives the desired identity for Borel measurable indicator functions $\chi_B$ and then by linearity for Borel simple functions $\sum_{n=1}^N a_n\chi_{B_n}$. Finally, $\mu_u(\Omega)=\int_{\Omega}f\,dx<\infty$ allows us to conclude formula \eqref{eq:weighted} for bounded Borel functions, since we may invoke monotone convergence applied to positive and negative parts of $H$. This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:identity}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain, let $f_j\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative functions, and assume that $\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$. For $j=1,2$, let $u_j\in C(\overline\Omega)$ be an Alexandrov solution of the Dirichlet problem
\[
     \begin{cases}
    \det D^2u=f_j,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     u=\varphi,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega.
    \end{cases}
\]
If the extremal boundary slopes of the two solutions agree,
\[
    q_{u_1}(x)=q_{u_2}(x),\quad x\in \partial \Omega,
\]
then the integral identity
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:two-weighted}
 \int_\Omega H(\nabla u_1)f_1\,dx
 =\int_\Omega H(\nabla u_2)f_2\,dx
\end{equation}
holds for every bounded Borel function $H\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to {\mathbb R}$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
First, from Corollary~\ref{cor:D-equality} we deduce that
    \[
        \mathcal{L}^n(\mathrm{D}_{u_1}\mathbin \triangle \mathrm{D}_{u_2})=0.
    \]
    Thus, using Proposition~\ref{prop:weighted}, we get for any bounded Borel function $H\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to{\mathbb R}$
    \[
    \begin{split}
        \int_\Omega H(\nabla u_1)f_1\,dx&=\int_{\mathrm D_{u_1}}H(p)\,dp\\
        &=\int_{\mathrm D_{u_2}}H(p)\,dp\\
        &=\int_\Omega H(\nabla u_2)f_2\,dx.
    \end{split}
    \]
    This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:limit}
  Let $\Omega\subset{\mathbb R}^n$ be a bounded strictly convex $C^{1,1}$ domain, let $f\in L^1(\Omega)$ be nonnegative, and suppose that $\omega\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ is a fixed direction. Moreover, for each $t>0$, let $U_{f,t} \in C(\overline{\Omega})$ be the unique Alexandrov solution of \eqref{eq:rescaled-PDE}. Then
\[
 U_{f,t}\to\Phi_\omega
 \quad\text{uniformly on }\overline\Omega
\]
as $t\to \infty$. Furthermore, along every sequence $t_k\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-conv}
 \nabla U_{f,t_k}(x)\to P_\omega x
 \quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Let us first observe that the convex function $\Phi_\omega\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ is the unique Alexandrov solution of the Dirichlet problem
   \begin{equation}
   \label{eq:MA-zero-source-Phi}
       \begin{cases}
    \det D^2 U=0,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     U=\Phi_\omega,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega.
    \end{cases}
   \end{equation}
   Indeed, $\Phi_\omega$ satisfies
\[
 \nabla\Phi_\omega (x)=P_\omega x\quad\text{for each }x\in{\mathbb R}^n,
\]
which yields
\[
    \mu_{\Phi_\omega}(A)=\mathcal{L}^n(\nabla \Phi_\omega (A))=\mathcal{L}^n(P_\omega (A))\leq \mathcal{L}^n(\omega^\perp)=0
\]
for every set $A\subset{\mathbb R}^n$, where we used that $\omega^\perp$ is an $(n-1)$ dimensional subspace of ${\mathbb R}^n$.

On the other hand, since $f\in L^1(\Omega)$,
\[
 t^{-n}\int_B f\,dx\to 0\quad \text{as }t\to\infty
\]
for every Borel set $B\subset \Omega$. In particular, the source stability of the Monge--Ampère problem (Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-source-stability}) implies that 
\[
    U_{f,t}\to \Phi_\omega\quad\text{uniformly on }\overline{\Omega}.
\]
For a fixed sequence $t_k\to \infty$, let $\mathcal{N}_k\subset \Omega$ be a Borel set, which has vanishing Lebesgue measure and contains the nondifferentiability points of $U_{f,t_k}$ (see proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:weighted}). Hence, 
\[
    \mathcal{N}\vcentcolon = \bigcup_{k\in{\mathbb N}}\mathcal{N}_k
\]
is a Borel set with vanishing Lebesgue measure such that for each $k\in{\mathbb N}$ the function $U_{f,t_k}$ is differentiable on $\Omega\setminus \mathcal{N}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:gradient-convergence} \ref{conv-subgrad-2}, applied at every remaining point, gives the gradient convergence \eqref{eq:gradient-conv}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Fix $\omega\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ and let $t_k\vcentcolon = t_k^\omega\to \infty$ be the sequence
from the hypothesis of the theorem. By \eqref{eq:countably-equal-slopes}, the two solutions $u_{j,k}\vcentcolon = u^{\omega}_{j,t_k^\omega}$, $j=1,2$, of \eqref{eq:MA-main-thm} have
the same one-sided convex boundary slopes $q_{u_{j,k}}$, $j=1,2$. As in Section~\ref{sec:large-amplitude-bdry-values}, the scaling property of the Monge--Ampère measures (Proposition~\ref{prop:scaling} \ref{scaling}) and the well-posedness theorem of the Monge--Ampère equation (Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-existence}) show that the convex function $U_{j,k}\vcentcolon = t_k^{-1}u_{j,k}\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ is the unique solution of 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rescaled-main}
    \begin{cases}
    \det D^2 U=t_k^{-n} f_j,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     U=\Phi_\omega,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega,
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}
for $j=1,2$. Moreover, Proposition~\ref{def:slope} and the scaling property $\partial (\lambda u)=\lambda \partial u$, for each $\lambda>0$, show that $q_{U_{j,k}}=t_{k}^{-1} q_{u_{j,k}}$, $j=1,2$, and hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:equal-slopes-reduced}
    q_{U_{1,k}}=q_{U_{2,k}}\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega,\quad k\in{\mathbb N}.
\end{equation}
Let $\psi\in C_c(\omega^\perp)$ and apply
Corollary~\ref{cor:identity} to the two equations \eqref{eq:rescaled-main}, with
$t=t_k$, and with
\[
H\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to{\mathbb R},\quad H(p)\vcentcolon =\psi(P_\omega p).
\]
We obtain
\[
 t_k^{-n}\int_\Omega
 \psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{1,k})f_1\,dx
=t_k^{-n}\int_\Omega
 \psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{2,k})f_2\,dx.
\]
After cancelling $t_k^{-n}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:prelimit}
 \int_\Omega\psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{1,k})f_1\,dx
 =\int_\Omega\psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{2,k})f_2\,dx.
\end{equation}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:limit}, we have
\[
 \nabla U_{j,k}(x)\to P_\omega x
 \quad\text{for a.e. }x \in \Omega
\]
as $k\to\infty$. Since $\psi$ is bounded, $f_j\in L^1(\Omega)$, and $P^2_\omega = P_\omega$, dominated convergence in \eqref{eq:prelimit} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:projection-identity}
 \int_\Omega\psi(P_\omega x)(f_1-f_2)(x)\,dx=0.
\end{equation}
Now, extend $g=f_1-f_2$ by zero to an $L^1$ function on ${\mathbb R}^n$. Each $x\in {\mathbb R}^n$ can be written uniquely as $x=x_\perp+ x_\parallel$ with $x_\perp\vcentcolon = P_\omega x \in \omega^\perp$ and $x_\parallel=s\omega$ for some $s\in{\mathbb R}$. Thus, Fubini's theorem allows us to rewrite \eqref{eq:projection-identity} as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:int-id}
 \int_{\omega^\perp}\psi(x_\perp )
 \left(\int_{\mathbb R} g(x_\perp+s\omega)\,ds\right)dx_\perp =0.
\end{equation}
By Fubini's theorem, the \emph{$X$-ray transform}
\[
    X_\omega g(x_\perp)\vcentcolon =\int_{\mathbb R} g(x_\perp+s\omega)\,ds
\]
is well-defined for a.e.~$x_\perp \in \omega^\perp$. Indeed, $g\in L^1({\mathbb R}^n)$ and Fubini's theorem yield
\[
    X_\omega g\in L^1(\omega^\perp)\quad\text{with}\quad \|X_\omega g\|_{L^1(\omega^\perp)}\leq \|g\|_{L^1({\mathbb R}^n)}.
\]
Since \eqref{eq:int-id} holds for every $\psi\in C_c(\omega^\perp)$, the fundamental lemma of the calculus of variations yields
\[
 X_\omega g(x_\perp) =\int_{\mathbb R} g(x_\perp+s\omega)\,ds=0
 \quad\text{for a.e. }x_\perp\in\omega^\perp.
\]
For every fixed vector $\eta\in\omega^\perp$, multiplying $\int_{\mathbb R} g(x_\perp+s\omega)\,ds$ by $e^{-i x_\perp\cdot \eta}$ and then integrating it over $\omega^\perp$ yields
\[
 \mathcal F_{\omega^\perp}(X_\omega g)(\eta)
 \vcentcolon =\int_{\omega^\perp}\int_{{\mathbb R}}g(x_\perp +s\omega)e^{-i x_\perp \cdot\eta}\,ds\,dx_\perp
 =\widehat g(\eta),
\]
where $\widehat{g}$ denotes the Fourier transform of $g$ in ${\mathbb R}^n$ and the last equality follows from $g\in L^1({\mathbb R}^n)$, Fubini's theorem, and $x\cdot \eta= x_\perp \cdot \eta$. This is the Fourier-slice identity; see
\cite[Chapter~I, Section~2]{Helgason}. Consequently
\[
    \widehat g(\eta)=0\quad \text{whenever $\eta\in{\mathbb R}^n$ satisfies $\eta\cdot\omega=0$.}
\]
Since $\omega\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ is arbitrary and for  every vector $\eta\in{\mathbb R}^n$ one can choose a unit vector $\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ such that $\omega\perp\eta$, we deduce that $\widehat g (\eta)= 0$ for all $\eta\in{\mathbb R}^n$. As the Fourier transform is injective on
$L^1({\mathbb R}^n)$, we get $g=0$ almost everywhere.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Fix $\omega\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}$ and $t>0$. The pair
\[
 (t\Phi_\omega|_{\partial\Omega},q_{u_{1,t}^\omega})
\]
belongs to $\mathcal C_{f_1}^{\mathrm{glob}}$ and therefore to
$\mathcal C_{f_2}^{\mathrm{glob}}$. Here and throughout the proof, $u_{j,t}^\omega \in C(\overline{\Omega})$ for  $j=1,2$, $t>0$, and $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$, denotes the unique Alexandrov solution to the Monge--Ampère problem with source $f_j$ and boundary value $t\Phi_\omega$ (see~Proposition~\ref{prop:forward} \ref{forward-existence}). Uniqueness of the Alexandrov Dirichlet problem for the source $f_2$ implies that the element of the latter data set with
Dirichlet component $t\Phi_\omega|_{\partial\Omega}$ is
$(t\Phi_\omega|_{\partial\Omega},q_{u_{2,t}^\omega})$.  Consequently
$q_{u_{1,t}^\omega}=q_{u_{2,t}^\omega}$.  Theorem~\ref{thm:cylindrical},
applied for instance with $t_k=k$, gives $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
First, Proposition~\ref{prop:boundary-Lipschitz} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:radial-slope} yield
\[
\Lambda_{f_j}^-(\varphi)
=
q_{u_{f_j,\varphi}}
\qquad\text{on }\partial\Omega
\]
for $j=1,2$ and every
$\varphi\in C^{1,1}(\partial\Omega)$. In particular, for every $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ and $t>0$,
the extremal boundary slopes corresponding to the boundary value
$t\Phi_\omega|_{\partial\Omega}$ agree. Theorem~\ref{thm:cylindrical}, applied with $t_k^\omega=k$, therefore gives $f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
We first record the weighted change of variables formula. Let $u\colon\Omega\to\mathbb R$ be convex and suppose that it solves
\[
 \vartheta (\nabla u)\det D^2 u=f\quad \text{in }\Omega
\]
in the Alexandrov sense, where $f\in L^1(\Omega)$ is nonnegative. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted-MA-change-of-variables}
 \int_\Omega H(\nabla u(x))f(x)\,dx
 =
 \int_{\mathrm D_u}H(p)\vartheta(p)\,dp
\end{equation}
for every bounded Borel function
$H\colon\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R$.

Indeed, let $\mathcal N\subset\Omega$ be a Borel set of Lebesgue measure zero containing the nondifferentiability set of $u$. Since $\mu_u^\vartheta=f\,dx$ as Borel measures in $\Omega$, we have
\[
 0
 =\int_{\mathcal{N}}f\,dx=
 \mu_u^\vartheta(\mathcal N)
 =
 \int_{\partial u(\mathcal N)}\vartheta(p)\,dp.
\]
Here and below, we use the observation that $\partial u(A)$ is Lebesgue measurable, whenever $A\subset \Omega$ is Borel (see~\cite[Theorem~2.3, Step~1]{Figalli}). Thus, we can use formula \eqref{eq:equal-Lebesgue-set} with $B=\partial u(A)$ for every Borel set $A\subset\Omega$. 

Using the lower bound \eqref{eq:bounds-weight-vartheta}, we infer from the previous identity that
\[
 \mathcal L^n(\partial u(\mathcal N))=0.
\]
Consequently, up to a Lebesgue null set,
$\mathrm D_u\vcentcolon =\partial u(\Omega)$ agrees with
$\nabla u(\Omega\setminus\mathcal N)$. Repeating the argument in the
proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:weighted}, with
$\vartheta(p)\,dp$ in place of $dp$, gives
\[
 (\nabla u)_\#(f\,dx)
 =
 (\vartheta\,\mathcal L^n)\mathbin{\vrule height 1.6ex depth 0pt width
0.13ex\vrule height 0.13ex depth 0pt width 1.3ex}\mathrm D_u
\]
as Borel measures, where $\nabla u$ may be defined arbitrarily on
$\mathcal N$. This proves
\eqref{eq:weighted-MA-change-of-variables}.

Now fix $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ and abbreviate
$t_k:=t_k^\omega$ and
\[
 u_{j,k}:=u_{j,t_k}^\omega,
 \qquad
 U_{j,k}:=t_k^{-1}u_{j,k},
 \qquad j=1,2.
\]
The equality of the boundary slopes and
Corollary~\ref{cor:D-equality} imply
\[
 \mathcal L^n\bigl(
 \mathrm D_{u_{1,k}}\mathbin\triangle
 \mathrm D_{u_{2,k}}
 \bigr)=0.
\]
Therefore, by \eqref{eq:weighted-MA-change-of-variables},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:weighted-MA-equal-integrals}
 \int_\Omega H(\nabla u_{1,k})f_1\,dx
 =
 \int_\Omega H(\nabla u_{2,k})f_2\,dx
\end{equation}
for every bounded Borel function $H$.

We next determine the large-amplitude limit. Since
\[
 \partial U_{j,k}(A)
 =
 t_k^{-1}\partial u_{j,k}(A),
\]
the ordinary Monge--Amp\`ere measure of $U_{j,k}$ satisfies, for
every Borel set $A\subset\Omega$,
\begin{align*}
 \mu_{U_{j,k}}(A)
 &=
 t_k^{-n}\mathcal L^n(\partial u_{j,k}(A))\\
 &\leq
 \frac{t_k^{-n}}{c_\vartheta}
 \int_{\partial u_{j,k}(A)}\vartheta(p)\,dp\\
 &=
 \frac{t_k^{-n}}{c_\vartheta}
 \int_A f_j(x)\,dx.
\end{align*}
In particular,
\[
 \mu_{U_{j,k}}(\Omega)
 \leq
 \frac{t_k^{-n}}{c_\vartheta}
 \|f_j\|_{L^1(\Omega)}
 \to 0.
\]
Consequently, $\mu_{U_{j,k}}\overset{\ast}{\rightharpoonup}0$ as $k\to\infty$. Moreover,
\[
 U_{j,k}=\Phi_\omega
 \quad\text{on }\partial\Omega.
\]
Hence, $U_{j,k}\in C(\overline{\Omega})$ are Alexandrov solutions of the Dirichlet problem
\[
\begin{cases}
\det D^2U=\nu_{j,k}
&\text{in }\Omega,\\
U=\Phi_\omega
&\text{on }\partial\Omega,
\end{cases}
\]
where we put $\nu_{j,k}\vcentcolon = \mu_{U_{j,k}}$. The source-stability result in Proposition~\ref{prop:forward}\,\ref{forward-source-stability} therefore gives
\[
 U_{j,k}\to\Phi_\omega
 \quad\text{uniformly on }\overline\Omega.
\]
Here we used that $\Phi_\omega$ is the unique convex solution of 
 \begin{equation}
       \begin{cases}
    \det D^2 U=0,\quad&\text{in }\Omega,\\
     U=\Phi_\omega,\quad&\text{on }\partial\Omega;
    \end{cases}
   \end{equation}
see proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:limit}. Lemma~\ref{lem:gradient-convergence} now yields
\[
 \nabla U_{j,k}(x)
 \to
 \nabla\Phi_\omega(x)=P_\omega x
 \quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega.
\]
Given $\psi\in C_c(\omega^\perp)$, apply
\eqref{eq:weighted-MA-equal-integrals} with
\[
 H_k(p):=\psi\left(\frac{P_\omega p}{t_k}\right).
\]
Since $\nabla u_{j,k}=t_k\nabla U_{j,k}$, we obtain
\[
 \int_\Omega
 \psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{1,k})f_1\,dx
 =
 \int_\Omega
 \psi(P_\omega\nabla U_{2,k})f_2\,dx.
\]
Dominated convergence gives
\[
 \int_\Omega
 \psi(P_\omega x)(f_1-f_2)(x)\,dx=0.
\]
As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:cylindrical}, this means that the
X-ray transform of the zero extension of $f_1-f_2$ vanishes in the
direction $\omega$. Since $\omega\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ was arbitrary,
the Fourier-slice identity implies
$f_1=f_2$ almost everywhere in $\Omega$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{thm:weighted-MA-uniqueness} is conditional on the
existence of the convex solutions $u_{j,t}^\omega$. It therefore
separates the inverse argument from the forward Dirichlet theory for
the weighted measure \eqref{eq:weighted-MA-measure}. Whenever the
weighted Dirichlet problem is well posed, the theorem gives uniqueness
of the source from the resulting cylindrical boundary-slope data.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:second-order-boundary-geometry}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a bounded domain with
$C^{1,1}$ boundary, and suppose that there exists $\kappa>0$ such
that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:quadratic-boundary-separation}
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)
\leq
-\kappa|y-x|^2
\qquad
\text{for every }x\in\partial\Omega
\text{ and }y\in\overline\Omega.
\end{equation}
Then there exists a set
$N\subset\partial\Omega$ satisfying $\mathcal H^{n-1}(N)=0$ such that the Gauss map $\nu:\partial\Omega\to\mathbb S^{n-1}$ is tangentially differentiable at every $\xi\in \partial\Omega\setminus N$.

At every $\xi\in \partial\Omega\setminus N$, the second fundamental form
\[
\mathrm{II}_\xi(\tau,\sigma)
:=
D_\tau\nu(\xi)\cdot\sigma,
\qquad
\tau,\sigma\in T_\xi\partial\Omega,
\]
is well-defined and satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:II-lower}
\mathrm{II}_\xi(\tau,\tau)
\geq
2\kappa|\tau|^2,
\qquad
\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
More precisely, for every $\xi\in \partial\Omega\setminus N$ and
$\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega$, there exists a boundary curve
$\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\partial\Omega$ that is twice
differentiable at the origin and satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:boundary-curve-second-order}
\gamma(0)=\xi,
\qquad
\gamma'(0)=\tau,
\qquad
\mathrm{II}_\xi(\tau,\tau)
=
-\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Since $\partial\Omega$ is of class $C^{1,1}$, its Gauss map is Lipschitz continuous. Rademacher's theorem \cite[Chapter~3]{evans1991measure}, applied in local boundary
charts, therefore shows that $\nu$ is tangentially differentiable
at $\mathcal H^{n-1}$-almost every point of $\partial\Omega$.
Denote the set of non-differentiable points by $N$.

Let us describe the corresponding second-order boundary expansion.
After a rigid change of coordinates, a neighborhood of a boundary
point can be represented as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:local-rep}
\partial\Omega\cap\mathcal U
=
\left\{
X(x')=(x',g(x')):x'\in V
\right\},
\end{equation}
where $V\subset\mathbb R^{n-1}$ is open and
$g\in C^{1,1}(V)$. Choosing the orientation so that the normal is
outward-pointing, the Gauss map is given by
\[
\nu(X(x'))
=
\frac{(-\nabla g(x'),1)}
{\sqrt{1+|\nabla g(x')|^2}}.
\]
The map
\[
q\longmapsto
\frac{(-q,1)}{\sqrt{1+|q|^2}}
\]
is smooth and has a smooth inverse on the relevant open
hemisphere. Consequently, tangential differentiability of $\nu$ at
$\xi=X(x_0')$ is equivalent to differentiability of
$\nabla g$ at $x_0'$. 

Thus, $\nabla g$ is differentiable on the set
\[
    \mathcal{V}\vcentcolon = X^{-1}((\partial\Omega\setminus N)\cap \mathcal{U}).
\]
For all $x'_0\in \mathcal{V}$, we have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-g-second-order}
\nabla g(x_0'+h)
=
\nabla g(x_0')+D^2g(x_0')h+o(|h|)
\qquad\text{as }h\to 0.
\end{equation}
Moreover, $g\in C^{1,1}(V)$ implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:fund-calc}
g(x_0'+h)-g(x_0')
=
\int_0^1\nabla g(x_0'+th)\cdot h\,dt
\end{equation}
whenever the segment
$[x_0',x_0'+h]$ is contained in $V$. Note that the expansion
\eqref{eq:gradient-g-second-order}, applied with $th$ in place of
$h$, is uniform for $t\in[0,1]$. Indeed, the differentiability of $\nabla g$ at $x'_0$ gives
\[
\nabla g(x_0'+k)
=
\nabla g(x_0')+D^2g(x_0')k+r(k),
\qquad
r(k)=o(|k|).
\]
Thus, for any $\delta>0$, we have
\[
|r(k)|\leq\delta|k|
\]
whenever $|k|$ is sufficiently small. Hence, for sufficiently small
$h$ and every $t\in[0,1]$,
\[
|r(th)|\leq\delta t|h|\leq\delta|h|.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\sup_{0\leq t\leq1}|r(th)|=o(|h|),
\]
yielding the asserted uniform convergence. Combining this convergence result with \eqref{eq:fund-calc} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:g-second-order-expansion}
g(x_0'+h)
=
g(x_0')+\nabla g(x_0')\cdot h
+\frac12D^2g(x_0')[h,h]+o(|h|^2).
\end{equation}
Thus the boundary admits a second-order expansion at every point of
$\partial\Omega\setminus N$.

Fix $\xi=X(x_0')\in \partial\Omega\setminus N$ and
$\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega$. Since
\[
DX(x_0'):
\mathbb R^{n-1}\to T_\xi\partial\Omega
\]
is an isomorphism, there exists a unique $v\in\mathbb R^{n-1}$ such that $DX(x_0')v=\tau$. For sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$, define
\[
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\partial\Omega,
\qquad
\gamma(s):=X(x_0'+sv).
\]
The expansion \eqref{eq:g-second-order-expansion} shows that
$\gamma$ is twice differentiable at $s=0$, with
\[
\gamma(0)=\xi,
\qquad
\gamma'(0)=\tau,
\qquad
\gamma''(0)=D^2X(x_0')[v,v].
\]
In particular,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:curve-second-order-expansion}
\gamma(s)-\xi
=
s\tau+\frac{s^2}{2}\gamma''(0)+o(s^2)
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:curve-distance-expansion}
|\gamma(s)-\xi|^2
=
s^2|\tau|^2+o(s^2).
\end{equation}

Applying \eqref{eq:quadratic-boundary-separation} with
$x=\xi$ and $y=\gamma(s)$ gives
\[
\nu(\xi)\cdot\bigl(\gamma(s)-\xi\bigr)
\leq
-\kappa|\gamma(s)-\xi|^2.
\]
Since $\nu(\xi)\cdot\tau=0$, equations
\eqref{eq:curve-second-order-expansion} and
\eqref{eq:curve-distance-expansion} yield
\[
\frac{s^2}{2}\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0)+o(s^2)
\leq
-\kappa s^2|\tau|^2+o(s^2).
\]
Dividing by $s^2/2$ and letting $s\to0$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:normal-curve-bound}
-\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0)
\geq
2\kappa|\tau|^2.
\end{equation}

Finally, differentiating
\[
\nu(\gamma(s))\cdot\gamma'(s)=0
\]
at $s=0$ gives
\[
D_\tau\nu(\xi)\cdot\tau
+
\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0)=0.
\]
Therefore
\[
\mathrm{II}_\xi(\tau,\tau)
=
D_\tau\nu(\xi)\cdot\tau
=
-\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0).
\]
Combining this identity with \eqref{eq:normal-curve-bound} proves
\eqref{eq:II-lower}.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Assume first that \ref{unif-convex-2} holds. Let us define the function
\[
\psi\colon U\to{\mathbb R},\quad \psi(x)\vcentcolon =\rho(x)-\frac{c_0}{2}|x|^2.
\]
Since $\rho\in C^{1,1}(U)$, its almost-everywhere Hessian $D^2 \rho$ agrees with its weak Hessian (see \cite[Section~3.1.2]{evans1991measure}). Hence, the function $\psi\in C^{1,1}(U)$ satisfies
\[
D^2\psi=D^2\rho-c_0I\geq0
\qquad\text{a.e. in }U,
\]
and therefore also in the sense of distributions. Since $U$ is
convex, it follows that $\psi$ is convex. 

We briefly justify the last implication. Since
$\psi\in C^{1,1}(U)$, \cite[Section~4.3.2]{evans1991measure} ensures that
$\psi\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2,\infty}(U)$, and by Rademacher's theorem its almost-everywhere Hessian represents its weak Hessian. Fix $x,y\in U$. Since $U$ is an open convex set, the compact segment $[x,y]$ is contained in $U$, and hence
\[
d:=\operatorname{dist}([x,y],\mathbb R^n\setminus U)>0.
\]
For $0<\varepsilon<d/2$, let
\[
\psi_\varepsilon:=\psi*\eta_\varepsilon
\]
on a neighborhood of $[x,y]$, where $\eta_\varepsilon$ is a standard nonnegative mollifier. Then
\[
D^2\psi_\varepsilon
=
(D^2\psi)*\eta_\varepsilon\geq0
\]
pointwise. Thus, the smooth function
\[
h_\varepsilon(t)
:=
\psi_\varepsilon((1-t)x+ty),
\qquad 0\leq t\leq1,
\]
satisfies
\[
h_\varepsilon''(t)
=
(y-x)^TD^2\psi_\varepsilon((1-t)x+ty)(y-x)
\geq0.
\]
It is well-known that this implies 
\[
\psi_\varepsilon((1-t)x+ty)
\leq
(1-t)\psi_\varepsilon(x)+t\psi_\varepsilon(y),\quad\text{for all }0\leq t\leq 1.
\]
By \cite[Section~4.2.1]{evans1991measure} and $\psi\in C(U)$, we have $\psi_\varepsilon\to \psi$ uniformly on compact subsets of $U$, and hence passing to the limit as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ yields
\[
\psi((1-t)x+ty)
\leq
(1-t)\psi(x)+t\psi(y).
\]
Since $x,y\in U$ and $t\in[0,1]$ were arbitrary, $\psi$ is convex.

As a consequence of the convexity of $\psi$, Remark~\ref{rem:rel-subgrad-grad} implies that
\[
\psi(y)\geq \psi(x)+\nabla \psi(x)\cdot(y-x),
\qquad x,y\in U.
\]
Substituting the definition of $\psi$ and rearranging, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:strong-convexity-rho}
\rho(y)\geq
\rho(x)+\nabla\rho(x)\cdot(y-x)
+\frac{c_0}{2}|y-x|^2,
\qquad x,y\in U.
\end{equation}
Differentiable functions satisfying an estimate of this type are said to be strongly convex and they are in particular convex. Thus, every sublevel set of $\rho$ is convex, and therefore
$\Omega=\{\rho<0\}$ is convex.

Moreover, $\nabla\rho$ does not vanish on $\partial\Omega$. Indeed, if $x\in\partial\Omega$ and $\nabla\rho(x)=0$, then
\eqref{eq:strong-convexity-rho} would imply
\[
\rho(y)\geq\frac{c_0}{2}|y-x|^2>0
\qquad
\text{for every }y\in U\setminus\{x\},
\]
contradicting $\rho|_{\partial\Omega}=0$.

Furthermore, the conditions \eqref{eq:strong-defining-function} imply that $\nabla \rho$ points in the exterior normal direction on the boundary:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-defining-fct}
\nabla\rho(x)=|\nabla\rho(x)|\nu(x),
\qquad x\in\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
Indeed, since $\rho\in C^{1,1}(U)$ and $\rho|_{\partial\Omega}=0$, the tangential components of $\nabla \rho$ are identically zero and only the normal component of $\nabla \rho$ survives. Using $\nabla \rho\neq 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, we find
\[
    \frac{\nabla \rho(x)}{|\nabla \rho(x)|}=\pm \nu(x).
\]
Suppose that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:contradiction-sign}
    \nabla \rho(x)=-|\nabla \rho(x)|\nu(x).
\end{equation}
Then $\rho(x)=0$, $x\in \partial\Omega$, implies that
\[
    \rho(x-s\nu(x))\geq s|\nabla \rho(x)|+\frac{c_0}{2}s^2>0,
\]
for all sufficiently small $s>0$. This contradicts $\rho(y)<0$ in $\Omega$, since $x-s\nu(x)\in \Omega$ for small $s>0$. 

Hence, we conclude the validity of \eqref{eq:gradient-defining-fct}. Therefore, recalling that $\nabla \rho\neq 0$ on $\partial\Omega$, this also establishes the representation formula \eqref{eq:rep-normal} of the normal field $\nu$.

For $x\in\partial\Omega$ and $y\in\overline\Omega$, \eqref{eq:strong-convexity-rho} and $\rho(y)\leq 0 = \rho(x)$ yield
\[
0\geq\rho(y)
\geq\nabla\rho(x)\cdot(y-x)
   +\frac{c_0}{2}|y-x|^2.
\]
Consequently, \eqref{eq:gradient-defining-fct} ensures that
\[
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)
\leq
-\frac{c_0}{2|\nabla\rho(x)|}|y-x|^2.
\]
Since $\partial\Omega$ is compact and $\nabla\rho$ is continuous on the neighborhood $U$,
condition \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-convexity} follows with
\[
\kappa_\Omega
=\frac{c_0}
       {2\|\nabla\rho\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)}}.
\]

Conversely, assume \ref{unif-convex-1}. We first show that the geometric condition \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-convexity} implies that $\Omega$ is convex. For each $x\in\partial\Omega $, define the closed half-space
\[
H_x^-
:=
\left\{
y\in\mathbb R^n:
\nu(x)\cdot(y-x)\leq0
\right\}.
\]
We claim that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:representation-om}
\overline\Omega
=
\bigcap_{x\in\partial\Omega}H_x^-.
\end{equation}
The inclusion ``$\subset$'' follows immediately from
\eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-convexity}. To prove the reverse inclusion,
suppose that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:choice-y}
y\in
\bigcap_{x\in\partial\Omega}H_x^-
\setminus\overline\Omega.
\end{equation}
Since $\overline\Omega$ is compact and $y\notin \overline{\Omega}$, there exists
$x_0\in\overline\Omega$ such that
\[
0<|y-x_0|
=
\operatorname{dist}(y,\overline\Omega).
\]
Necessarily we have $x_0\in\partial\Omega$. Indeed, if $x_0\in \Omega$, then there exists $0<\varepsilon<1$ such that $x_\varepsilon\vcentcolon = x_0+\varepsilon (y-x_0)\in \Omega$ and 
\[
    |y-x_\varepsilon|=(1-\varepsilon)|y-x_0|<|y-x_0|=\operatorname{dist}(y,\overline{\Omega}),
\]
contradicting minimality of $|y-x_0|$.

Let $\tau\in T_{x_0}\partial\Omega$ be a tangent vector and choose a $C^1$ curve $\gamma\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\partial\Omega$ satisfying
\[
\gamma(0)=x_0,
\qquad
\gamma'(0)=\tau.
\]
Since $x_0$ minimizes the distance from $y$ to $\overline\Omega$, the function $s\to |y-\gamma(s)|^2$ has a minimum at $s=0$. Therefore,
\[
0
=
\left.\frac{d}{ds}\right|_{s=0}|y-\gamma(s)|^2
=
-2(y-x_0)\cdot\tau.
\]
Since this holds for every
$\tau\in T_{x_0}\partial\Omega$, the vector $y-x_0$ is normal to $\partial\Omega$ at $x_0$. Consequently,
\[
y-x_0=t\nu(x_0)
\]
for some $t\in\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}$. We claim that $t>0$.
Indeed, if $t<0$, then $y-x_0$ points in the inward normal
direction. Since $\partial\Omega$ is of class $C^1$, it follows that
\[
x_0+\varepsilon(y-x_0)\in\Omega
\]
for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon>0$. But then
\[
\bigl|y-\bigl(x_0+\varepsilon(y-x_0)\bigr)\bigr|
=(1-\varepsilon)|y-x_0|
<\operatorname{dist}(y,\overline\Omega),
\]
which is impossible. Hence $t>0$, and therefore
\[
\nu(x_0)\cdot(y-x_0)=t>0.
\]
Thus $y\notin H_{x_0}^-$, contradicting the choice of $y$. This
proves \eqref{eq:representation-om}.

Now, since the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:representation-om} is an intersection of closed half-spaces, $\overline\Omega$ is convex. Moreover, because $\Omega$ is a domain with $C^1$ boundary,
\[
    \Omega=\operatorname{int}(\overline\Omega).
\]
The interior of a convex set is convex, and hence $\Omega$ is
convex.

We now proceed with the construction of the defining function $\rho$. Fix a point $x_\ast\in\Omega$ and define
\[
\Omega_*:=\Omega-x_\ast.
\]
For notational convenience, we continue to denote by $\nu$ the
outward unit normal to $\partial\Omega_*$. It is related to the
original normal field on $\partial\Omega$ by translation.

The translated set $\Omega_*$ contains the origin, $0\in \Omega_*$. Let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Minkowski}
p\colon {\mathbb R}^n\to [0,\infty],\qquad p(z):=\inf\{t>0:t^{-1}z\in \Omega_*\}
\end{equation}
be the Minkowski functional induced by $\Omega_*$. By \cite[Lemma~1.2]{Brezis}, $p$ has the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
    \item\label{vanishing} \textit{Vanishing at the origin}: $p(0)=0$,
    \item\label{pos-hom} \textit{Positive homogeneity}: $p(\lambda x)=\lambda p(x)$, for all $x\in {\mathbb R}^n$ and $\lambda>0$.
    \item\label{sub-add} \textit{Subadditivity}: $p(x+y)\leq p(x)+p(y)$, for all $x,y\in{\mathbb R}^n$.
    \item\label{continuity-p} \textit{Continuity}: There exists $C>0$ such that $0\leq p(x)\leq C|x|$ for all $x\in{\mathbb R}^n$. 
    \item\label{unit-p-ball} \textit{Unit ball}: $\Omega_*=\{p<1\}$.
\end{enumerate}
The properties \ref{pos-hom}, \ref{continuity-p}, and \ref{unit-p-ball} ensure that 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Minkowski-on-bdry}
    \partial \Omega_*=\{p=1\}.
\end{equation}
The inclusion $\partial \Omega_*\subset \{p=1\}$ is immediate, since if $p(z)>1$ or $p(z)<1$ for some $z\in \partial\Omega_*$, then, by continuity \ref{continuity-p}, we would have $p<1$ or $p>1$ on some open ball $B$ containing $z$. In each case, \ref{unit-p-ball} yields a contradiction. On the other hand, if $p(z)=1$, then \ref{pos-hom} implies
\[
p((1-\varepsilon)z)<1<p((1+\varepsilon)z),
\]
so every neighborhood of $z$ meets both $\Omega_\ast$ and its
complement. Thus, $z\in \partial\Omega_*$, which shows $\{p=1\}\subset \partial\Omega_*$.

Furthermore, since $0\in\Omega_\ast$ and $\Omega_\ast$ is bounded, there exist constants $0<r<R<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:balls-omega-star}
\overline B_r
\subset
\Omega_\ast
\subset
\overline B_R.
\end{equation}
Let $\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast$. Since the uniform convexity condition \eqref{eq:geometric-uniform-convexity} is invariant under translations, we again have
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:translated-unif-conv}
\nu(\xi)\cdot(z-\xi)\leq -\kappa_\Omega |z-\xi|^2
\qquad
\text{for every }z\in\overline\Omega_\ast.
\end{equation}
Taking $z=r\nu(\xi)\in\overline B_r\subset\Omega_\ast$, we obtain
\[
r-\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)
=
\nu(\xi)\cdot\bigl(r\nu(\xi)-\xi\bigr)
\leq0.
\]
On the other hand, since
$\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast\subset\overline B_R$,
the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives
\[
\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\leq|\xi|\leq R.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gauge-transversality}
r\leq\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\leq R,
\qquad
\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast.
\end{equation}

Next, let us define the exit time functional $\ell\colon \mathbb S^{n-1}\to [0,\infty)$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:exit-time}
\ell(\theta)
:=
\sup\left\{
t>0:t\theta\in\Omega_\ast
\right\},\quad \theta\in \mathbb{S}^{n-1}.
\end{equation}
Then condition \eqref{eq:balls-omega-star} implies that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:bounds-l}
r\leq\ell(\theta)\leq R.
\end{equation}
Moreover, convexity of $\Omega_\ast$,
$0\in\Omega_\ast$, and definition \eqref{eq:exit-time} imply that
\[
\Omega_\ast\cap\{t\theta:t\geq0\}
=
\{t\theta:0\leq t<\ell(\theta)\}.
\]
Consequently, every ray emanating from the origin intersects
$\partial\Omega_\ast$ in precisely one point, namely
$\ell(\theta)\theta$. Conversely, for every boundary point $\xi\in\partial\Omega_*$, there is a unique ray starting at the origin and passing through that point, which necessarily has velocity vector $\theta \vcentcolon =\xi/|\xi|\in\mathbb S^{n-1}$. 

Thus the radial projection
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:radial-projection}
\pi\colon\partial\Omega_\ast\to\mathbb S^{n-1},
\qquad
\pi(\xi)\vcentcolon =\frac{\xi}{|\xi|},
\end{equation}
is bijective, with $\pi^{-1}(\theta)=\ell(\theta)\theta$. 

We next verify the regularity of $\pi^{-1}$. For
$\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast$, set $\widehat\xi:=\frac{\xi}{|\xi|}$. The transversality estimate \eqref{eq:gauge-transversality} and the bound
$|\xi|\leq R$ give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:uniform-radial-transversality}
\widehat\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)
=
\frac{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)}{|\xi|}
\geq\frac rR>0.
\end{equation}
In particular, the radial direction $\widehat\xi$ does not belong to
$T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast$.

The differential of the smooth map
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:def-phi}
    \Phi\colon {\mathbb R}^n\setminus\{0\}\to {\mathbb R}^n,\quad \Phi(x)=\frac{x}{|x|}
\end{equation}
at the point $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*$ is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diff-Phi}
D\Phi(\xi)v
=
\frac1{|\xi|}
\left(I-\widehat\xi\otimes\widehat\xi\right)v,\quad v\in{\mathbb R}^n,
\end{equation}
which is the orthogonal projection onto $\widehat \xi^\perp$ scaled by $|\xi|^{-1}$. Hence its kernel is $\operatorname{span}\{\xi\}$. Using the chain rule, we see that $D\pi(\xi) \colon
T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast
\to
T_{\widehat\xi}\mathbb S^{n-1}$ is the restriction of $D\Phi(\xi)$ to $T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast$. Since \eqref{eq:uniform-radial-transversality} implies that $D\pi(\xi)$ is injective, the rank--nullity theorem yields that $D\pi(\xi)$ is an isomorphism. Indeed, let $\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast$ and decompose $\widehat \xi$ as
\[
\widehat\xi
=
\bigl(\widehat\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\bigr)\nu(\xi)
+
\widehat\xi_T,
\qquad
\widehat\xi_T\in T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast.
\]
Then $\tau\cdot\nu(\xi)=0$ and $\widehat \xi \in\mathbb S^{n-1}$ yield
\[
\widehat\xi\cdot\tau
=
\widehat\xi_T\cdot\tau\quad\text{and}\quad|\widehat\xi_T|^2
=
1-\bigl(\widehat\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\bigr)^2.
\]
Consequently, the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives
\[
|\widehat\xi\cdot\tau|^2=|\widehat\xi_T\cdot \tau|^2
\leq
\left(
1-\bigl(\widehat\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\bigr)^2
\right)|\tau|^2.
\]
It follows that
\begin{align*}
\left|
\left(I-\widehat\xi\otimes\widehat\xi\right)\tau
\right|^2&=|\tau-(\widehat \xi\cdot\tau)\widehat\xi|^2\\
&=
|\tau|^2-|\widehat\xi\cdot\tau|^2\\
&\geq
\bigl(\widehat\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\bigr)^2|\tau|^2.
\end{align*}
Using \eqref{eq:uniform-radial-transversality}, we therefore obtain
\[
|D\pi(\xi)\tau|
=
\frac{1}{|\xi|}
\left|
\left(I-\widehat\xi\otimes\widehat\xi\right)\tau
\right|
\geq
\frac{r}{R^2}|\tau|.
\]
In particular, the linear map $D\pi(\xi)
\colon
T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast
\to
T_{\widehat\xi}\mathbb S^{n-1}$ is injective, as we have asserted.

Since $\partial\Omega_\ast$ is of class $C^{1,1}$ and map $\Phi$ is smooth, the radial projection $\pi$ is a $C^{1,1}$ map between $C^{1,1}$ manifolds. This can also be seen directly from formula \eqref{eq:diff-Phi}. Moreover, as $D\pi(\xi)$, $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*$, is an isomorphism, the
$C^{1,1}$ inverse function theorem, applied in local boundary charts, shows that $\pi$ admits a locally $C^{1,1}$ inverse near every point. Because $\pi$ is bijective, these local inverses agree with its set-theoretic inverse
\[
\pi^{-1}(\theta)=\ell(\theta)\theta,
\qquad
\theta\in\mathbb S^{n-1}.
\]
Consequently, $\pi^{-1}\colon
\mathbb S^{n-1}\to\partial\Omega_\ast$ is of class $C^{1,1}$. Since $\ell(\theta)=\pi^{-1}(\theta)\cdot\theta$, it follows that $\ell\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb S^{n-1})$.

Finally, using the definitions of the Minkowski functional $p$ and the exit time functional $\ell$ (see \eqref{eq:Minkowski}, \eqref{eq:exit-time}), together with property positive homogeneity of $p$ \ref{pos-hom}, we find 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gauge-radial-representation}
p(z)
=|z|p(z/|z|)=\frac{|z|}{\ell(z/|z|)},\qquad z\neq 0.
\end{equation}
Since $\ell\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb S^{n-1})$ and
$\ell\geq r>0$ (see~\eqref{eq:bounds-l}), formula
\eqref{eq:gauge-radial-representation} shows that $p\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
\bigl(\mathbb R^n\setminus\{0\}\bigr)$.

Next, let us introduce the function
\[
F\colon{\mathbb R}^n\to [0,\infty],\quad F(z):=\frac12p(z)^2.
\]
Then \ref{pos-hom} implies that $F$ is positively homogeneous of degree two. To prove that $F\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb R^n)$, note that $p\in C^{1,1}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb R}^n\setminus\{0\})$ implies that $\nabla F\in C^{0,1}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb R}^\setminus\{0\})$. Thus, the positive homogeneity of $\nabla F$ yields the existence of a constant $C'>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-F-est}
|\nabla F(z)|\leq C'|z|.
\end{equation}
Thus $\nabla F$ extends continuously to the origin by 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:nabla-F-origin}
    \nabla F(0)=0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, since $F(0)=0$ and
\[
0\leq F(z)=\frac12p(z)^2\leq\frac{C^2}{2}|z|^2
\]
(see \ref{vanishing} and \ref{continuity-p}), the function $F$ is differentiable at the origin with
$\nabla F(0)=0$. Moreover, the one-homogeneity of $\nabla F\in C({\mathbb R}^n)$ and the local Lipschitz continuity
\[
\nabla F\in C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,1}
(\mathbb R^n\setminus\{0\})
\]
give $\nabla F\in C^{0,1}({\mathbb R}^n)$. Indeed, let $x,y\in\mathbb R^n$, and put $M=\max\{|x|,|y|\}$. If $M=0$, then 
\[
    |\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)|=0\leq C |x-y|
\]
for any $C>0$ and hence we may assume that $M>0$. If $|x-y|\geq M/2$, then \eqref{eq:gradient-F-est}--\eqref{eq:nabla-F-origin} yield
\[
|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)|
 \leq C'(|x|+|y|)\leq 2C'M
 \leq 4C'|x-y|.
\]
If $|x-y|\leq M/2$, then both $x/M$ and $y/M$ belong to the annulus
\[
    \mathcal{A}\vcentcolon = \{z\in{\mathbb R}^n:\,1/2\leq |z|\leq 1\}.
\]
The lower bound follows from the triangle inequality and the choice $M=\max(|x|,|y|)$. Thus positive homogeneity of $\nabla F$ and $\nabla F\in C^{0,1}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb R}^n\setminus\{0\})$ yield
\[
\begin{aligned}
|\nabla F(x)-\nabla F(y)|
&=M\left|
\nabla F\left(\frac{x}{M}\right)
-\nabla F\left(\frac{y}{M}\right)
\right| \\
&\leq C_{\mathcal A}|x-y|.
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore $\nabla F$ is globally Lipschitz and
$F\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb R^n)$.

Thus, it remains to show that $F$ is uniformly convex, that is, it satisfies 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:uniform-conv-F}
    D^2 F\geq cI\quad\text{a.e. in }{\mathbb R}^n.
\end{equation}

By \eqref{eq:Minkowski-on-bdry}, $F$ satisfies $F=1/2$ on
$\partial \Omega_*$ and hence the tangential components of $\nabla F$ vanish on $\partial \Omega_*$. This shows that $\nabla F|_{\partial\Omega_*}$ points into the normal direction to $\partial \Omega_*$, that is, for all $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:nablaF-bdry}
    \nabla F(\xi)=s(\xi)\nu(\xi)\quad\text{for some }s(\xi)\in{\mathbb R}.
\end{equation}
Since $F$ is two-homogeneous, Euler's theorem gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:Eulers-id}
    2F(z)=\nabla F(z)\cdot z,\qquad z\neq 0.
\end{equation}
Thus, $F=1/2$ on $\partial\Omega_*$ and \eqref{eq:nablaF-bdry} imply 
\[
    1=s(\xi)(\nu(\xi)\cdot \xi).
\]
Thus, \eqref{eq:uniform-radial-transversality} yields
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-gauge}
    \nabla F(\xi)=\frac{\nu(\xi)}{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)},\qquad \xi\in\partial \Omega_*.
\end{equation}
By homogeneity,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-gauge-ray}
\nabla F(t\xi)
=t\frac{\nu(\xi)}{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)},
\qquad t>0,\quad \xi\in\partial \Omega_*.
\end{equation}

Now, let $N\subset\partial \Omega_*$ be the set from Lemma~\ref{lem:second-order-boundary-geometry}. We already know that $\mathcal H^{n-1}(N)=0$ and hence the product
$[1/j,j]\times N$ has zero $n$-dimensional Hausdorff measure. Now, for each $j\in\mathbb N$, the restriction of the radial parametrization $\Psi$,
\[
\Psi|_{[1/j,j]\times\partial \Omega_*}\colon [1/j,j]\times\partial \Omega_*\to {\mathbb R}^n,\quad (t,\xi)\mapsto t\xi,
\] 
is Lipschitz, so the image of $[1/j,j]\times N$ has zero
$n$-dimensional Lebesgue measure \cite[Chapter~2]{evans1991measure}. Taking the union over $j$ shows
that the cone generated by $N$ is Lebesgue-null, that is, 
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cone-gen-N}
    \mathcal{L}^n(\{t\xi:t>0,\xi\in N\})=0.
\end{equation}
Thus, if we show that $F$ is twice differentiable at every point on the ray generated by a good point $\xi \in \partial\Omega_*\setminus N$, then by \eqref{eq:cone-gen-N} $F$ is twice differentiable almost everywhere in ${\mathbb R}^n$.

To prove differentiability on the good rays, define
\[
A(\xi):=
\frac{\nu(\xi)}{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)},
\qquad \xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast.
\]
Then equation~\eqref{eq:gradient-gauge-ray} becomes
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:gradient-radial-coordinates}
\nabla F(t\xi)=tA(\xi).
\end{equation}
Next fix $\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast\setminus N$. Since $\nu$ is
tangentially differentiable at $\xi$ and
$\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\geq r>0$, the map $A$ is tangentially
differentiable at $\xi$. More precisely, for every
$\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast$,
\[
D_{\partial\Omega_\ast}A(\xi)[\tau]
=
\frac{D_\tau\nu(\xi)}{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)}
-
\frac{\xi\cdot D_\tau\nu(\xi)}
     {(\xi\cdot\nu(\xi))^2}\nu(\xi),
\]
where we have used $\tau\cdot\nu(\xi)=0$. Let $X\colon V\subset\mathbb R^{n-1}
\to\partial\Omega_\ast$ be a local $C^{1,1}$ parametrization with $X(x'_0)=\xi$ (cf.~\eqref{eq:local-rep}), and set
\[
\widetilde\Psi\colon (0,\infty)\times V\to {\mathbb R}^n,\quad\widetilde\Psi(s,x'):=sX(x').
\]
Its differential satisfies
\[
D\widetilde\Psi(t,x'_0)[a,h]
=
a\xi+tDX(x'_0)h.
\]
Since $\mathbb R^n
=
\operatorname{span}\{\xi\}
\oplus T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast$
this differential is invertible and hence
$\widetilde\Psi$ is a local $C^{1,1}$ diffeomorphism at $(t,x'_0)$. In these coordinates, \eqref{eq:gradient-radial-coordinates} becomes
\[
\nabla F\bigl(\widetilde\Psi(s,x')\bigr)
=
sA(X(x'))
=:\widetilde G(s,x').
\]
The map $\widetilde G$ is differentiable at $(t,x'_0)$ because $X$ is $C^{1,1}$ at $x'_0$, $\nu\circ X$ is differentiable at $x'_0$, and $\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)\geq r>0$. Therefore 
\[
\nabla F
=
\widetilde G\circ\widetilde\Psi^{-1}
\]
is differentiable at $t\xi=\widetilde\Psi(t,x'_0)$. Consequently, $F$ is twice differentiable at $t\xi$, as we wanted to see.

Finally, let $\gamma$ be the twice differentiable curve from Lemma~\ref{lem:second-order-boundary-geometry} passing through a point $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*\setminus N$ into the direction $\tau\in T_\xi \partial\Omega_*$. Since $F=\frac12$ on $\partial\Omega_\ast$, we have $F(\gamma(s))=\frac12$, for small $s$. Since $F$ is twice differentiable at $\xi$, differentiating this identity twice at $s=0$ gives
\[
0
=
D^2F(\xi)[\tau,\tau]
+
\nabla F(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0).
\]
Using \eqref{eq:boundary-curve-second-order}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:lower-bound-tangential-second}
\begin{split}
D^2F(\xi)[\tau,\tau]
&=
-\frac{\nu(\xi)\cdot\gamma''(0)}
       {\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)}=
\frac{\mathrm{II}_\xi(\tau,\tau)}
     {\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)}\geq 
\frac{2\kappa_\Omega}{R}|\tau|^2,
\qquad
\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Moreover, differentiating Euler's identity \eqref{eq:Eulers-id} at $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*\setminus N$ gives 
\[
    D^2F(\xi)\xi=\nabla F(\xi).
\]
Hence, \eqref{eq:gradient-gauge} implies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:second-ord-xi-xi}
D^2F(\xi)[\xi,\xi]=1,
\qquad
D^2F(\xi)[\xi,\tau]=0
\quad
\text{for }\tau\in T_{\xi}\partial \Omega_*.
\end{equation}
Now, let $v\in\mathbb R^n$ be an arbitrary vector. By \eqref{eq:gauge-transversality}, we have $\xi\notin T_\xi \partial\Omega_*$ and thus
\[
\mathbb R^n
=
\operatorname{span}\{\xi\}
\oplus
T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast.
\]
Consequently, $v\in{\mathbb R}^n$ can be decomposed as
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:radial-tangential-decomposition}
v=a\xi+\tau,
\qquad
a\in\mathbb R,
\quad
\tau\in T_\xi\partial\Omega_\ast.
\end{equation}
More explicitly,
\[
a
=
\frac{v\cdot\nu(\xi)}
{\xi\cdot\nu(\xi)},
\qquad
\tau=v-a\xi.
\]
The transversality estimate
\eqref{eq:gauge-transversality} gives
\[
|a|
\leq
\frac{|v|}{r},
\qquad
|\tau|
\leq
|v|+|a||\xi|
\leq
\left(1+\frac Rr\right)|v|.
\]
Thus the decomposition is uniformly nondegenerate.

Using
\eqref{eq:lower-bound-tangential-second} and
\eqref{eq:second-ord-xi-xi}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
D^2F(\xi)[v,v]
&=
a^2D^2F(\xi)[\xi,\xi]
+2aD^2F(\xi)[\xi,\tau]
+D^2F(\xi)[\tau,\tau]\\
&\geq
a^2+\frac{2\kappa_\Omega}{R}|\tau|^2.
\end{align*}
Set
\[
\lambda_0
:=
\min\left\{1,\frac{2\kappa_\Omega}{R}\right\}.
\]
Then
\[
D^2F(\xi)[v,v]
\geq
\lambda_0\bigl(a^2+|\tau|^2\bigr).
\]
On the other hand, since $|\xi|\leq R$,
\[
|v|
\leq
|a||\xi|+|\tau|
\leq
R|a|+|\tau|
\leq
\sqrt{R^2+1}\,
\bigl(a^2+|\tau|^2\bigr)^{1/2}.
\]
It follows that
\[
a^2+|\tau|^2
\geq
\frac{|v|^2}{R^2+1}.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:second-diff-a.e.-bdry-point}
D^2F(\xi)[v,v]
\geq
c|v|^2,
\qquad
c
:=
\frac{1}{R^2+1}
\min\left\{1,\frac{2\kappa_\Omega}{R}\right\}>0,
\end{equation}
for every $v\in\mathbb R^n$ and every
$\xi\in\partial\Omega_\ast \setminus N$. 

Finally, since $\nabla F$ is positively homogeneous of degree one, we have
\[
\nabla F(tz)=t\nabla F(z),
\qquad t>0.
\]
Now we can differentiate this identity with respect to $z$ at $\xi\in \partial\Omega_*\setminus N$, which yields
\[
D^2F(t\xi)=D^2F(\xi).
\]
Therefore, \eqref{eq:second-diff-a.e.-bdry-point} implies
\[
D^2F(t\xi )\geq cI
\qquad\text{for all }t>0,\,\xi\in\partial\Omega_*\setminus N.
\]
The complement of these good rays is contained in the cone
generated by $N$, together with the origin. By
\eqref{eq:cone-gen-N}, this exceptional set has zero Lebesgue
measure. Consequently,
\[
D^2F\geq cI
\qquad\text{a.e. in }\mathbb R^n.
\]

Finally, let us set
\[
\rho(x)\vcentcolon =2F(x-x_*)-1=p(x-x_\ast)^2-1.
\]
We deduce from the properties of $F$ that $\rho$ belongs to $C^{1,1}(\mathbb R^n)$ and satisfies
\[
\Omega=\{\rho<0\},\qquad
\partial\Omega=\{\rho=0\},\qquad
D^2\rho\geq2cI
\quad\text{a.e. in }\mathbb R^n.
\]
This proves \ref{unif-convex-2}.
